Score 5: Concrete, measurable, publicly visible. "She went from $80K in revenue to $340K in 18 months," with a named case study.

Score 3-4: Visible but partially abstract. "Clients report significant improvement in team communication" — real but not fully verifiable.

Score 1-2: Abstract and soft. "Clients experience greater clarity and peace of mind." Unmeasurable and unprovable.

Score 0: No outcomes visible anywhere.

**Sub-variable P — Payment Normalisation (0 to 5):**

Payment normalisation asks whether people in this world already spend money on things adjacent to this offer. Is the act of paying for this type of service already a normal behaviour in this culture, or are you asking buyers to create a new spending category?

Score 5: Normal and expected. "Executive coaching for senior leaders" — the corporate world has fully normalised paying for executive coaches.

Score 3-4: Somewhat established. "Content strategy consulting for SaaS companies" — some companies do this but it is not universal.

Score 1-2: Novel spending category. "Cognitive performance coaching for professional gamers" — the audience exists but the paying-for-coaching behaviour is not yet established.

Score 0: No adjacent spending behaviour exists.

**The gate logic:**

A raw OV score below 10 (out of 20), mapping to below 5 on the /10 scale, triggers a hard cap: the entire composite score is capped at 40 out of 100. This is not a soft penalty. It is a structural ceiling. No amount of brilliant identity stacking, native language, or strong carrier trust will push the total above 40 if the offer itself cannot pass the gate.

The practitioner's instruction when this gate triggers: "You cannot niche your way out of a non-viable offer. Before we work on positioning, we need to work on offer structure. Here is what the data says is missing from the viability assessment."

**Common mistakes in scoring Offer Viability:**

The most common mistake is conflating the practitioner's belief in the offer with market evidence of demand. A passionate founder who has personally experienced the problem they solve will often score their own D variable at 5 when the honest score is 2. The rule is: D must be supported by external evidence — actual forum posts, actual competitor offers, actual search data — not by the practitioner's conviction.

The second common mistake is treating category confusion as a communication problem rather than an offer design problem. If buyers consistently misunderstand what the offer is, the instinct is to "communicate it better." Often the problem is that the offer genuinely spans two categories and needs to choose one.

**Diagnostic questions for Offer Viability:**
- Is there documented external evidence of active demand — actual forum posts, competitor offers, search data — or only the practitioner's belief?
- Can the buyer categorise this offer in under five seconds without further explanation?
- Where can you point to someone whose success with this offer is publicly observable and verifiable?
- Are there examples of buyers in this culture already spending money on adjacent services at this price point and format?
- If demand is latent rather than active, what is the realistic timeline and investment required to educate the market before the offer can be purchased?

---

### Engine 2: Identity Match — The Paint Job (Layer 1 Component)

**What it measures:** How many identity layers are stacked in the positioning and how densely they overlap to create immediate recognition by the ideal client.

**The six identity layers:**

1. **Subculture Identity** — The tribe the ideal client belongs to, defined by their voluntary group membership and what that membership says about who they are. Not "small business owners" but "immigrant founders building without family money." Not "HR professionals" but "HR Business Partners who trained in engineering before moving into people operations." Subculture is the most recognition-generating layer because it is self-chosen and emotionally loaded. People feel a strong membership identity in their subculture.

2. **Local Culture Identity** — Geographic or institutional identity that creates in-group recognition. "Melbourne-based" or "NHS-trained" or "ex-Big 4" creates immediate shorthand recognition for people inside those worlds. The test is whether the location or institution has a strong shared culture — not just a place, but a sensibility that insiders immediately recognise.

3. **Temporal Identity** — The specific season of life, career, or business the ideal client is in. "In the first 24 months of practice ownership" or "post-exit, figuring out what's next" or "returning to the workforce after five years on parental leave." Temporal identity is powerful because it maps to specific urgencies. The person in their first 24 months has different burning problems than the person in year seven.

4. **Psychic Identity** — The internal experience that people feel but rarely say aloud. The things they think about at 3am. The quiet frustration they cannot explain to their partner. "The person who knows they are more capable than their current results reflect" is psychic identity. "The advisor who has stopped believing their own pitch about growth potential" is psychic identity. This layer resonates most deeply because it names what others will not.

5. **Aspirational Identity** — Who the ideal client is in the process of becoming. Not the achieved identity but the emerging one. "The founder who eventually wants to step back and have the business run without her" is aspirational identity. This layer is powerful when paired with temporal identity because it creates a narrative arc — where they are now, where they are heading, what gap exists.

6. **Algorithmic Identity** — The content ecosystem, feed cluster, and recommendation bubble the ideal client inhabits. What accounts they follow, what hashtags they engage with, what algorithm categories they fall into. This layer informs content format and distribution strategy more than positioning language, but naming it explicitly matters when the niche is defined by content consumption habits as much as professional identity.

**Scoring:**

Score each layer present on a 0-5 scale for how explicitly, densely, and correctly it is used. The top three layers in the positioning are the active ones. Total score out of 15 is then normalised to a contribution to the Layer 1 total.

0-2 per layer: layer is nominally present but thinly deployed.
3-4: layer is present and doing recognizable work.
5: layer is fully deployed — it creates immediate, specific recognition.

**Score band descriptions:**

12-15 / 15 (maps to 8-10 / 10 in the composite): Three layers densely stacked. The positioning stops the specific right person with precision. Wrong-fit prospects self-select out without being told to.

8-11 / 15 (maps to 5-7 / 10): One or two layers working, with gaps. The positioning stops some right people but the aperture is too wide.

0-7 / 15 (maps to 0-4 / 10): Generic or near-generic. The positioning is for "everyone in this general space" rather than a specific type.

**Worked example at each band:**

*Strong (14/15):* A financial coach targeting "women in their 40s going through divorce who have never managed household finances before and are realizing for the first time that they don't know where the money went." This stacks temporal identity (40s, going through divorce), psychic identity (the shame of realizing they've never managed money), and subculture identity (women navigating financial independence for the first time). Every layer is dense and specific. Score: 14/15.

*Middle (9/15):* A business coach helping "creative agency owners scale their team." Subculture identity (creative agency owners) is present and specific. Temporal identity (scaling — implies a growth stage) is partially present. Psychic and aspirational layers are absent. The positioning will stop some right people but could be sharpened significantly. Score: 9/15.

*Weak (4/15):* A coach helping "business owners build a better brand." No subculture specificity (any business owner). No temporal identity. No psychic layer. No aspirational specificity. This is a beige house with a sign that says "this is a house." Score: 4/15.

**Diagnostic questions:**

- Name the identity layers present in this positioning. Are any of them generic (e.g., "entrepreneurs") when they could be specific (e.g., "bootstrapped SaaS founders in their second year")?
- Which layer produces the strongest emotional recognition in your best-fit clients? Is it dominant in the positioning?
- What is the psychic layer — the thing your ideal client thinks but never says publicly? Is it in the positioning?

**Common mistakes:**

Using size or demographic filters as identity layers. "$10M-$50M revenue companies" is not identity — no founder wakes up feeling a strong identity connection to their ARR band. "Bootstrapped founders who got to $5M without outside investment and are now wondering if they made a mistake" is identity — it maps to a specific emotional experience.

**Diagnostic questions for Identity Match:**
- Name every identity layer present in the positioning. Are any of them demographic filters (size, revenue, title) rather than genuine identity layers?
- Which layer produces the strongest emotional recognition in the best-fit clients? Is that layer dominant in the positioning?
- What is the psychic identity layer — the thing the ideal client thinks but never says publicly? Is it named anywhere in the positioning?
- What temporal identity is implied? What specific season of life or business is the ideal client in right now?
- If you read this positioning aloud to a room of one hundred broadly-fitting candidates, would the right ten lean forward immediately — or would eighty think it might be for them?

---

### Engine 3: Language Match — The Window (Layer 1 Component)

**What it measures:** Whether the language used in content, profiles, and offers matches the actual internal vocabulary of the ideal client's daily professional and emotional experience.

**The five language layers:**

1. **Functional Language** — The verbs and nouns of daily work. What the ideal client does, what tools they use, what their routines look like. "Pulling the pipeline report," "prepping for a QBR," "building the deck for the board pack," "jumping on a Notion call" — these are functional language fragments. Using them correctly signals that you have been inside their working day. Using them incorrectly — or using synonyms that nobody in this world actually uses — signals the opposite.

2. **Technical Language** — Insider vocabulary: acronyms, jargon, frameworks, and terminology that only people inside this world know and use correctly. In financial services: "NAV," "redemption queue," "drawdown period." In startup: "ARR," "churn," "CAC:LTV," "runway." In healthcare: "MDT," "NICE guidelines," "referral pathway." Technical language is the membership test. Using it correctly signals insider status. Getting it wrong — or using the wrong version of a term for the regional variant of the industry — is immediately disqualifying.

3. **Emotional Language** — How this world feels to the people inside it. Not the sanitized professional description of challenges, but the actual emotional texture. Not "navigating a complex transition" but "the specific dread of opening your email on Monday morning when you know there is going to be something from that client." Emotional language is the mirror layer — it shows the buyer that you understand not just their situation but their interior experience of it.

4. **Aspirational Language** — The specific words and phrases the ideal client uses for the future they want. Not "increased revenue" but "getting off the tools" or "having a business that runs without me in it" or "being able to say no to clients I don't like without panicking." Aspirational language is specific to subculture — the way a startup founder describes their desired exit looks nothing like the way a physiotherapy practice owner describes their desired lifestyle shift, even if both involve "financial freedom."

5. **Cultural and Colloquial Language** — Slang, shorthand, shared references, community-specific phrases and in-jokes. "The Slack graveyard" (channels nobody uses anymore), "founder ADHD" (the inability to stay focused on one initiative), "the valley of death" (the $1M-$3M ARR dip). These phrases emerge organically inside communities and are invisible from outside. They represent the highest-trust language layer because using them correctly signals that you are not just studying this world — you are of it.

**The noun removal test revisited:**

Take a piece of content. Remove all specific nouns. Read what is left. If what remains sounds generic — if the sentence structure, emotional register, and cultural rhythm could describe any industry — the language is not native. Native language is specific at the level of sentence texture, not just vocabulary.

"When you lose a deal at final stages because procurement got involved and changed the terms three weeks before close" — remove the nouns (deal, final stages, procurement, terms, close) and you still have the emotional specificity and structural texture of enterprise sales culture. The frustration is embedded in the grammar, not just the vocabulary.

"When you face challenges in your business journey" — remove "challenges" and "business journey" and there is nothing left. The sentence is empty of meaning at every level.

**Scoring:**

Score the top two language layers on 0-5 each. Total out of 10.

0-3: Generic or near-generic. Language is borrowed from marketing conventions, not from the niche's actual vocabulary.
4-6: Mixed. Some native language present but diluted with generic phrasing.
7-8: Mostly native. Functional and emotional layers working. Minor gaps in technical or cultural layers.
9-10: Unmistakably native. All relevant layers present. Noun removal test passes.

**Worked examples at different score levels:**

*Score 9/10 — B2B SaaS sales training:* "The moment your SDR sends 180 emails in a day and books zero meetings, and you sit there knowing the sequence is the problem but not being able to articulate exactly which step is breaking the flow — that's the SDR-to-AE handoff gap nobody talks about in training programmes." Native functional language (SDR, AE, sequence, handoff), technical vocabulary deployed correctly, emotional specificity (the frustration of knowing the problem but not its source), cultural register matching the intensity of SaaS sales culture.

*Score 5/10 — Leadership coaching:* "I help leaders navigate complex environments and develop the skills needed to build high-performing teams." Some functional language (teams, leaders) but the verbs (navigate, develop, build) are generic. No technical language. No emotional specificity. No cultural texture.

*Score 2/10 — Business consulting:* "Unlock your organisation's true potential through strategic alignment and transformational change." Language-shaped noise. No niche specificity at any layer. Fails noun removal test completely.

**Common mistakes:**

Borrowing language from competitor content rather than from primary sources inside the community. Competitor content is already translated through the filter of marketing convention. The raw, unfiltered language of the community is found in forums, comment sections, DMs, and informal conversation — not in polished competitor copy.

Using technical language incorrectly. A single misused term can destroy credibility more effectively than using generic language throughout. If you use "SaaS" where someone in the industry would say "PaaS," or use "NAV" in a context where "unit price" is the term actually used, it signals that you have studied the language from outside rather than living it.

**Diagnostic questions for Language Match:**
- Apply the noun removal test to three pieces of content. After removing all specific nouns, does the sentence texture, emotional register, and specificity survive — or does the content collapse to generic?
- Which language layer is weakest: functional, technical, emotional, aspirational, or cultural/colloquial?
- Where did the specific phrases in this content come from — primary sources inside the community (forums, DMs, call transcripts), or competitor content already filtered through marketing convention?
- Could someone outside this world have written this content using desk research, or does it require genuine immersion to produce?
- Is technical vocabulary used correctly for this specific regional and sector variant — or is there any risk of a misused term that would signal outsider status to an insider?

---

### Engine 4: Cultural Match — The Inside Atmosphere (Layer 1 Component)

**What it measures:** Whether the positioning's tone, credibility signals, status language, and implied tempo align with the unspoken cultural rules of the niche.

**The four cultural dimensions:**

1. **Credibility Rules** — In some worlds, formal credentials are everything. In others, credentials are neutral or even slightly suspicious. In the tech startup ecosystem, a PhD from MIT is worth less than having built and sold a company. In the NHS, a published clinical trial is worth more than a hundred client testimonials. In the independent financial adviser world, real-world client results and peer reputation matter more than institutional affiliation. Misunderstanding the credibility rules of a world means presenting the wrong evidence in the wrong format — and the ideal client, sensing this, marks the positioning as off without being able to explain why.

2. **Status Rules** — What is respected in this world? What signals high status versus low status? In some worlds, naming clients is high status. In others, discretion is the mark of a serious professional and name-dropping is cringe. In some worlds, a premium price is a status signal. In others, being "accessible" matters and high prices read as extractive. In some worlds, technical depth earns status. In others, practical brevity earns more. The practitioner who understands the status rules of a niche knows how to frame success in a way that reads as aspirational rather than arrogant, and how to present pricing in a way that reads as legitimate rather than predatory.

3. **Taboo Rules** — Every culture has things you do not say, positions you do not take, and framings that immediately mark you as an outsider. In mental health professional communities, selling "wellness programmes" as a replacement for clinical care is taboo. In the corporate finance world, emotional or relational language in business contexts is taboo. In the developer community, selling before demonstrating value is taboo — you show the work first, always. In conservative industries, political signalling in either direction can be immediately disqualifying. Violating a taboo is the fastest way to lose a niche's trust irreversibly.

4. **Tempo Rules** — What pacing feels native? The startup world moves at the speed of a Twitter thread — short, punchy, immediate, high-frequency. The legal profession moves at the speed of a brief — long, precise, careful, slow-building. The executive coaching world for senior corporate leaders moves at the speed of a trusted conversation — warm, considered, private, long-horizon. Posting daily short-form content in the legal profession feels native to startups but alien and slightly aggressive in the wrong context. Creating long-form quarterly reports in the startup world signals that you misunderstand the pace of the world you are trying to serve.

**Scoring:**

Score the top 2.5 dimensions at up to 4 points each. Total out of 10.

0-3: Significant cultural misalignment. The positioning is visibly from outside the culture it serves.
4-6: Mostly aligned with notable gaps. The credibility and status signals are mostly right but taboo or tempo misalignment creates friction.
7-8: Strong alignment. Cultural rules respected across most dimensions.
9-10: Native cultural fluency. The positioning could have been produced by someone fully inside this world.

**Worked example — cultural mismatch:**

An American executive coach moves to Melbourne and tries to port his New York-inflected positioning to the Australian corporate market. In New York, bold claims ("world-class," "industry-leading," "transformational") are cultural currency. In Melbourne corporate culture, those claims read as American hubris. The Australian corporate world values understatement, humour, and results-without-trumpet. His content feels slightly wrong to Melbourne MDs even though the underlying expertise is genuinely excellent. Credibility rules (5/10 — competence respected but delivery is off), status rules (4/10 — claims are slightly cringe in this market), taboo rules (6/10 — no major violations but persistent minor friction), tempo (7/10 — acceptable pace). Cultural Match: 5.5/10.

**Worked example — cultural alignment:**

A former NHS GP who left clinical practice to consult to primary care networks on workforce planning. She knows the NHS from the inside. Her posts reference specific NHS structures (PCNs, ICSs, the QOF), credibility signals appropriate to the NHS world (clinical background + management experience, not external management consultancy credentials), and a tone that is direct, collaborative, and evidence-referencing. She does not sell aggressively — NHS culture finds hard selling distasteful. She offers value publicly and builds trust over time. Cultural Match: 9/10.

**Common diagnostic mistake:**

Assuming cultural rules transfer between adjacent niches. The rules in the startup SaaS world do not transfer to the scale-up world. The rules in the UK financial services sector do not transfer to the Australian financial services sector. The rules for B2B tech marketing do not transfer to B2B professional services marketing. Cultural rules are hyperlocal to the specific world being served.

**Diagnostic questions for Cultural Match:**
- If someone who genuinely belongs to this world walked into this profile or content, at what point would they feel a subtle wrongness? What would most likely trigger that feeling?
- What credibility signals does this world respect most? Are those signals present and prominent, or are different (less relevant) signals being used?
- Is there any status language that might read as arrogance in this culture? Or under-claiming that might undermine authority?
- What are the known taboos in this world? Does any element of the current positioning risk violating one?
- Does the tempo and format of content production match the natural rhythm of this world, or does it import the conventions of a different culture?

---

### Engine 5: Carrier Trust — The Person (Layer 2)

**What it measures:** Whether the person delivering this positioning is someone the ideal buyer feels psychologically safe following — assessed through seven specific observable signals, with Lived Credibility weighted double.

**The seven signals:**

**Signal 1 — Lived Credibility (weighted 2×, scores 0-10 in composite):**

Has this person genuinely been inside the world they serve? Not as an observer, not as an academic, not as an adjacent industry professional — but as a full participant who has experienced the daily friction, the cultural rules, the specific frustrations, and the specific victories of this world firsthand? Lived credibility is the foundational signal because it underpins every other trust signal. When it is present and genuine, language authenticity follows automatically. The right words come without effort because they were absorbed through experience. When it is absent, every other signal must be consciously constructed and, for perceptive buyers, the construction shows.

The diagnostic question: "Can you point to a specific period in your own life when you were the person you now serve? What did that period look like on a typical Tuesday?"

Score 8-10: Full immersion. Years inside the world, with experiences that map directly to the ideal client's present pain points.
Score 5-7: Partial immersion. Adjacent experience that provides credibility signals but with visible gaps.
Score 2-4: Studied from outside. Genuine research and engagement but no direct lived experience.
Score 0-1: No connection to the world being served.

**Signal 2 — Motive Transparency (0-5):**

Is it visible, from the content and positioning, why this person is in this niche? What drove them to it? What personal experience, failure, or transformation led them here? Opaque motives create a low-level suspicion that does not rise to a conscious objection but suppresses conversion nonetheless. The buyer wonders, without ever explicitly forming the question: what does this person actually want from me? When motives are transparent — especially when they are honest about vulnerability, failure, or a specific personal cost — that suspicion dissolves.

**Signal 3 — Constraint Alignment (0-5):**

Do the creator's real-world constraints match the buyer's? A business coach who has built and sold multiple companies has genuinely different constraints from a client who is in their second year, has three children, and is running the business from their kitchen table. If the creator's advice systematically assumes resources, time, risk tolerance, or support structures that the buyer does not have, the buyer will feel this as a form of being misunderstood — even if they cannot articulate exactly why the advice feels slightly wrong.

**Signal 4 — Consistency and Predictability (0-5):**

Does this person show up reliably and consistently over time? Trust is built through predictability more than through any single spectacular demonstration of quality. A practitioner who posts three times a day for two weeks and then goes silent for a month signals unreliability at a deep level, regardless of the content quality during the active period. Consistency of message — not varying wildly between topics, tone, or positions — also matters. Erratic messaging makes the underlying identity feel unstable.

**Signal 5 — Costly Signals (0-5):**

What has this person given up, invested, or risked to be in this position? Costly signals are the antidote to performative positioning. "I left a £140,000-a-year role to do this" is a costly signal. "I spent three years doing this work for free to build my understanding before charging anyone" is a costly signal. "I turned down X to stay focused on serving this specific community" is a costly signal. These signals cannot be faked because they involve observable sacrifice. When a costly signal is present and visible, it transforms the buyer's assessment from "this person says they're committed to this" to "this person has paid the price for this commitment."

**Signal 6 — Status Calibration (0-5):**

Does this person occupy the right position relative to their buyer — respected and elevated, but not untouchably superior? Both miscalibrations cause trust failures. Too high: the positioning reads as aspirational but unrelatable — the ideal client does not feel this person understands their actual daily situation. Too low: the positioning reads as a peer rather than a guide — the ideal client does not feel they would gain anything new by paying this person. The ideal calibration is "I've been where you are and I can see the path from here."

**Signal 7 — Values Clarity (0-5):**

Is it clear what this person stands for — and against? Values expressed only as positive ideals ("I believe in integrity, results, and partnership") do not produce the strong recognition that values expressed as positions do. Standing against something — a common practice in the industry, a dominant narrative, a specific type of bad advice — makes values feel real rather than decorative. "I don't believe in growth-at-all-costs" is a value. "I believe in sustainable growth" is a decoration. The former takes a position and creates productive polarisation.

**Scoring and gate:**

Sum all signals. Lived Credibility counts twice.
0-10: Low carrier trust. Buyers will feel an inexplicable reluctance that will suppress conversion regardless of offer quality.
10-17: Moderate carrier trust. Some signals working but visible gaps.
18-25: Strong carrier trust. Buyer feels psychologically safe following this person.

Gate: If Layer 2 total (post-calculation) is below 10 out of 25, Layers 3 and 4 scores are halved in the composite. Weak carrier trust prevents content and conversion mechanics from doing their work regardless of how well-crafted they are.

---

### Engine 6: Content Analysis — The Window Diagnostic (Layer 3)

**What it measures:** The quality and mechanism of the content itself — whether it functions as a genuine window that creates recognition, builds desire, and moves the reader toward engagement.

**The Window Test (four components):**

1. **Reflection** — Does the content show the reader their own world? Does reading it produce the felt experience of being seen? Reflection is the primary function of content in this model. Without it, everything else the content does is working upstream against a fundamental failure.

2. **Peek** — Does the content create curiosity about what is inside the house without showing everything? Content that teaches everything leaves nothing to want. The peek is the art of showing enough to be genuinely useful while simultaneously creating a felt gap — a sense that there is more here that the reader wants access to.

3. **Step-Through** — Is there a clear, natural path from this piece of content to the next level of engagement? This does not have to be an explicit CTA. In high-trust niches where hard CTAs are culturally inappropriate, the step-through is often embedded in the content itself — a natural implication that the reader who wants to go further knows where to go.

4. **Present Test** — Does this content work for the reader at the stage they are actually in? A post that describes the strategy for scaling past $5M ARR is useless to someone who has not yet hit their first $500K. The mismatch between the content's assumed stage and the reader's actual stage produces a specific type of non-engagement: the post is clearly valuable, but not for me, not right now.

**The Movie Trailer Test (four components):**

Great content functions like a great movie trailer — it shows enough to create desire without delivering the full experience.

1. **Cast and Setting (Genre)** — Does the reader immediately recognise the world being described? Within the first sentence, does the content signal clearly "this is for people in my world"?

2. **Story** — Is there a narrative arc, not just information? Information without narrative structure is reference material. Narrative creates the emotional engagement that makes content memorable and shareable.

3. **Conflict** — Is there visible stakes and tension? Every strong piece of content has a problem at its centre. The conflict does not have to be dramatic — it can be subtle and professional — but its presence is what gives the content energy. Content without conflict is flat.

4. **Director's Cut** — Is there a unique angle or perspective that only this specific person, with their specific lived experience and point of view, could deliver? The director's cut is the carrier trust signal embedded in the content itself. Generic content could have been written by anyone. Director's cut content carries a legible voice.

**The Virality Dimensions (five components):**

1. Hook Power — Does the opening line stop the scroll? Does it create immediate relevance or curiosity for the right reader?
2. Value Density — Is the insight-to-word ratio high? Does every sentence earn its place?
3. Emotional Pull — Does it produce a felt response? Not just a thought, but a feeling?
4. Authority Signal — Does consuming this content make the reader trust the creator more?
5. Conversion Bridge — Does it implicitly or explicitly move the reader toward wanting to engage further?

**Scoring:** Total Layer 3 Content Mechanics out of 20 (split across content quality, pain frequency alignment, magnetic polarity, format execution, and window test). The content analysis contributes approximately half of the Layer 3 score.

**Diagnostic questions for Content Analysis:**
- Run the four Window Tests on three recent pieces of content. Which test fails most consistently — Reflection, Peek, Step-Through, or Present Test?
- Run the Movie Trailer test on the opening sentence of the last five posts. Is the world, character, and stakes established within the first sentence?
- What is the insight-to-word ratio? Are there sentences that could be removed without losing meaning?
- What is the Director's Cut angle — the unique perspective only this specific creator, with their specific lived experience, could deliver? Is it present and consistent?
- Does the content create curiosity and desire without over-explaining? Where does it tend to show its hand too early?

---

### Engines 7 and 8: Trust Utility and Timing — The Conversion Variables

**Engine 7 — Trust Utility (TU):**

Formula: TU = P(outcome) × Value − Risk

Trust Utility asks whether the buyer can compute a positive expected value from engaging with this offer. It is not about whether the offer is good — it is about whether the buyer can assess that it is good before committing. The higher the P(outcome) as the buyer perceives it, and the higher the observable value of the outcome, and the lower the perceived risk, the higher the Trust Utility score.

The dominant forms of risk that buyers manage are financial (can I afford to be wrong about this?), reputational (what happens to my standing if I'm publicly associated with a failure?), time (can I afford the opportunity cost of this?), and identity (what does this choice say about who I am?). Different niches have different dominant risk types. Medical professionals worry primarily about reputational risk. Early-stage founders worry primarily about financial and time risk. Corporate executives worry about identity risk — what does choosing this coach say about me to my peers?

**Diagnostic questions for Trust Utility:**
- What type of risk is dominant for this specific buyer: financial, reputational, time, or identity?
- How visible and verifiable are the outcomes of this offer to someone who has not yet experienced it?
- What is the perceived cost of being wrong about this purchase decision — and is there anything in the positioning that reduces that perceived cost?
- What evidence is available before commitment (case studies, free value, peer testimonials) that allows the buyer to assess probability of success?
- Is the value of the outcome stated in terms this specific buyer will recognise — their language, their metrics, their aspirational state?

**Engine 8 — Timing and Pressure (T):**

Formula: T = P × C × M

P (Present Pain): Is the buyer in pain right now — not eventually, not theoretically, but today?
C (Cost of Delay): What is the visible, concrete cost of not solving this today versus waiting six months?
M (Moment of Change): Is there a triggering event — a new role, a failed launch, a regulatory change, a personal transition, a market shift — that makes right now a natural and urgent decision point?

The Timing engine identifies whether the positioning captures buyers at the moment of maximum readiness. Many offers are positioned in the abstract — "whenever you're ready to grow your business" — when the buyer only becomes ready to buy at specific, identifiable moments. An offer positioned against the moment of change rather than the general condition catches buyers when their buying energy is highest.

**Diagnostic questions for Timing and Pressure:**
- Is the buyer described as being in active pain right now, or is the positioning aimed at a hypothetical future readiness?
- What is the concrete, specific cost of not solving this problem today versus waiting six months? Is that cost made visible in the content?
- What triggering events (a new role, a failed launch, a regulatory change, a personal transition) make this problem urgent right now — and is the positioning calibrated to catch buyers at that moment?
- Is there a seasonal or cyclical moment in this niche when buying readiness peaks? Is the content timed accordingly?

---

### Engines 9 and 10: Pain Frequency Map and Magnetic Polarity

**Engine 9 — Pain Frequency Map:**

The Pain Frequency Map organises the pains addressed by the offer into three tiers by how frequently they surface in the buyer's experience:

*Daily Pains (Gold):* Problems the buyer encounters every single working day. These are the highest-priority content topics because they have a continuous queue of relevance. Every day, the buyer is in the situation described. "As an in-house recruiter, the daily pain of managing hiring manager expectations that outpace available talent" — this is a problem that surfaces every day.

*Weekly Pains:* Recurring but not daily. Often tied to weekly rhythms — end-of-week reporting, weekly team meetings, weekly client reviews.

*Monthly Pains:* Cyclical problems tied to monthly processes — billing cycles, monthly reporting, performance reviews, client renewal discussions.

*The Anchor Moment:* Each niche has one specific, recurring moment that concentrates the daily pain most acutely. For a service business owner, it might be the moment on Sunday afternoon when they start dreading Monday. For a hospital department head, it might be the quarterly budget review where all the under-resourcing becomes visible in a single document. Identifying and naming the anchor moment produces the most resonant hooks.

**Diagnostic questions for Pain Frequency Map:**
- Which tier of pain dominates the current content — daily, weekly, or monthly? Is this the tier with the highest buyer urgency?
- Has the anchor moment been specifically identified and named? Is it referenced explicitly in the content?
- Is there content deliberately targeting each pain frequency tier, or is the content concentrated entirely at one level?
- Are the daily pains described in enough operational specificity that a buyer would recognise their own Tuesday morning in the description?

**Engine 10 — Magnetic Polarity:**

Strong positioning does not just attract the right people. It actively repels the wrong ones. Magnetic polarity is the dual force of attraction and repulsion that makes a niche feel safe for its intended audience — because the wrong people are clearly not welcome.

Content with strong magnetic polarity contains signals that the ideal client will find welcoming and that a wrong-fit prospect will find mildly uncomfortable. An offer positioned specifically for "people who are willing to do the work and are tired of being told that mindset is the answer" will attract the action-oriented, evidence-seeking buyer and actively push away the mindset-coaching seekers. Neither is wrong — but the polarised positioning ensures the right buyer self-selects in and the wrong buyer self-selects out.

A useful diagnostic question: "If your wrong-fit clients read your content this week, would they recognise they are in the wrong place? Or would they feel perfectly at home?" If wrong-fit prospects feel at home in your content, your positioning lacks polarity.

**Diagnostic questions for Magnetic Polarity:**
- If the wrong-fit clients read this week's content, would they feel they are in the wrong place, or would they feel comfortable?
- What specific signal in the positioning tells the right person "this is specifically for someone like you"?
- What is being explicitly excluded, opposed, or stood against that creates safety and recognition for the ideal buyer?
- Does the content attract engagement from people who never buy — and if so, is that an audience-breadth problem or a polarity failure?

---

### Engines 11-15: Supporting Diagnostic Mechanisms

**Engine 11 — Recognition and Trust (RT) Composite:**

RT = I × L × C × K

This is not a separate scoring mechanism but the mathematical combination of the four core recognition and trust inputs. Identity Match (I), Language Match (L), Cultural Match (C), and Carrier Trust (K) are multiplied together to produce the RT force. Because it is multiplicative, a score of zero in any component collapses the composite to zero regardless of the others. A practitioner with perfect language match, strong cultural alignment, and high carrier trust will still produce a near-zero RT if their identity match is essentially absent — because the buyer will never stop to look at the window.

**Diagnostic questions for RT Composite:**
- Which of the four RT variables (I, L, C, K) scores lowest? That variable is the constraint — fixing the others will not move RT until that one improves.
- Is any variable at or near zero? If so, is this a gradient issue (weak but non-zero) or a binary failure (genuine zero from cultural violation or complete identity absence)?
- What does the multiplicative collapse look like with actual scores? What RT value would result from scoring all four components honestly?
- If RT is low overall, which variable is the most tractable to fix in the shortest time?

**Engine 12 — Hook / Movie Trailer:**

The hook is the first two seconds of any content encounter. It is evaluated through the Cast/Setting/Genre model: within the first sentence or frame, does the content instantly establish the world, the character, and the stakes?

The structural template: "You are [identity] and [situation]." This template works because it places the reader inside the content immediately rather than asking them to work out whether the content is for them.

Bad hook: "Here are 5 tips for growing your business."
Good hook: "If you've been running a photography business for three years and you still can't predict what next month's revenue will look like, this is for you."

The bad hook is generic and flat. The good hook uses temporal identity (three years), functional specificity (revenue predictability), and psychic resonance (the anxiety of unpredictable income) in a single sentence.

**Diagnostic questions for Hook / Movie Trailer:**
- Does the opening sentence place the ideal reader inside their own experience within the first five words?
- Which identity layer(s) are present in the hook — subculture, temporal, psychic, or other? Is the strongest available layer being used?
- Would a wrong-fit reader self-select out at the opening line, or would they read on because the hook is generic enough to seem relevant to them?
- Review the last five hooks. What percentage pass the Cast/Setting/Genre test — establishing world, character, and stakes in the first sentence?

**Engine 13 — Content Format (Coloring Book):**

Content formatted as a coloring book provides the outlines (a structure that matches the ideal client's world exactly) and leaves space for the reader to fill in the details from their own specific experience. The coloring book format works because it does not describe a generic version of the problem — it describes the specific structural shape of the problem and lets the reader's own experience provide the colour.

A coloring book post about client scope creep for agency owners does not describe "the challenge of managing client expectations." It describes the specific moment: "Your discovery call said six pages. The Figma file now has twenty-three screens. The client hasn't acknowledged the scope change but they've started cc'ing their CEO on emails." The practitioner has drawn the exact outline of a specific experience. The reader fills it in with their own version.

**Diagnostic questions for Content Format (Coloring Book):**
- Does the content describe the specific structural shape of the problem, or a generalised version that could describe dozens of different experiences?
- What specific, observable operational detail anchors this content to a real-world moment — a time, a tool, a specific action?
- Is there space in the content for the reader to fill in from their own experience, or does the content over-explain and close that space?
- Could this content have been written without having been inside this world — or does its specificity require immersion?

**Engine 14 — Inbound Lead Content:**

The content architecture is specifically designed to generate inbound enquiries, not just engagement. Three content functions must be present:
1. Content that creates recognition and attraction (the recognition function).
2. Content that builds trust progressively after the initial attraction (the trust function).
3. Content that creates a clear, frictionless path from trust to enquiry (the conversion function).

Most content is over-indexed on function 1 (recognition) and under-indexed on functions 2 and 3. A practitioner can generate significant engagement without any conversion infrastructure. The diagnostic checks whether all three functions are present and whether they work sequentially.

**Diagnostic questions for Inbound Lead Content:**
- Are all three content functions present — recognition, progressive trust-building, and conversion bridge? Which is most under-indexed?
- Is there a clear, sequenced progression that could take a new follower from first encounter to enquiry without requiring unusual initiative on their part?
- What is the conversion step embedded in or connected to the highest-performing recognition content? Is it calibrated to the buyer's current stage?
- If a highly motivated ideal client read the last ten posts, what would be their clear next step? If that step is unclear or absent, where does the funnel break?

**Engine 15 — Cold DM Precision:**

For outbound prospecting, the diagnostic assesses whether the opening message demonstrates genuine subculture fluency through the 3-Part Gift Rule:

1. **Unexpected** — The opener contains something genuinely useful that the recipient did not ask for and was not expecting. It demonstrates that the sender has paid attention to this specific person's specific situation.

2. **Useful** — The gift has immediate, practical value for this specific person. A generic tip is not a gift. An observation about a specific gap in their onboarding flow that you noticed during a trial of their product — that is a gift.

3. **Optional** — The message creates no obligation. It does not ask for anything in return. It is complete as a gift, without the implicit requirement that the recipient now owes the sender a conversation.

The 3-Part Gift Rule works because it reverses the normal outbound message dynamic (sender wants something from recipient) and replaces it with a value transfer that changes the social context of the interaction.

**Diagnostic questions for Cold DM Precision:**
- Does the opening message demonstrate that the sender has specifically studied this recipient — not just their industry, but their specific situation, content, or work?
- Is the gift immediately useful to this specific person, or is it a generic piece of value that could have been sent to anyone in the niche?
- Does the message create zero obligation? Would the recipient feel comfortable ignoring it without social cost or awkwardness?
- Read the message aloud as the recipient. Does it feel like a gift, or does it feel like a pitch wearing a gift's clothing?
- Is the subculture fluency demonstrated through the message's language and framing, not just its stated content?

---

## Section C: The Multiplicative Model — PL = OV × RT × TU × T

### Why Multiplicative, Not Additive

Most scoring systems used in positioning are additive. They assign weights to various components, sum the weighted scores, and produce a total. This seems logical — it feels fair to give credit where credit is due across multiple dimensions. But it creates a fundamental problem: an additive model allows excellence in one area to compensate for catastrophic failure in another.

Consider a practitioner who scores 9/10 on identity match, 9/10 on language quality, 8/10 on content mechanics, and 8/10 on carrier trust — but whose offer is essentially non-viable. In an additive model, the excellent scores across positioning dimensions would produce a respectable total, and the diagnostic might suggest "some offer refinement needed" while primarily focusing on the strong positioning work.

But this is structurally incorrect. The purchase likelihood is not the sum of positioning strengths minus offer weakness. It is the product of all variables. An offer nobody wants will not be purchased regardless of how perfectly positioned it is. The positioning strength is real — it exists — but it cannot produce the outcome (purchase) that justifies its existence without a viable offer at the centre.

The multiplicative model enforces this reality mathematically:

PL = OV × RT × TU × T

If OV = 0, the entire expression equals zero.
If RT = 0, the entire expression equals zero.
If TU = 0, the entire expression equals zero.
If T = 0, the entire expression equals zero.

No single variable's strength can compensate for another variable's failure. This is not a modelling choice — it is an accurate representation of how purchase decisions work. A buyer who encounters a perfect pitch for an offer they fundamentally do not want will not buy. A buyer who wants the offer but does not trust the person will not buy. A buyer who trusts the person and wants the offer but cannot see how to act right now will not buy today.

### Walking Through a Mathematical Collapse

Let us take a concrete example to show what this looks like in practice.

**Scenario:** A wellness coach wants to help "busy professionals reduce stress and improve their wellbeing through a twelve-week programme."

Offer Viability assessment:
- D (Latent Demand): There is demand for stress reduction, but not specifically for a twelve-week wellness programme at this price point. Demand is latent — professionals know they are stressed, but they do not actively seek wellness programmes. Score: 2/5
- C (Category Belief): "Wellness programme" is a recognisable category but in the corporate professional market, it is associated with employer-provided benefits, not individually purchased services. Score: 2/5
- O (Outcome Observability): The outcomes (less stress, better wellbeing) are abstract and not easily verifiable in others. Score: 2/5
- P (Payment Normalisation): Busy professionals are not an established paying cohort for individually purchased twelve-week wellness programmes at premium prices. Score: 1/5

OV raw score: 7/20 → maps to 3.5/10. This is below 5. Gate triggers. Total CAPPED at 40.

RT assessment:
- Identity Match: "Busy professionals" — single weak layer. Score: 4/10
- Language Match: "Reduce stress and improve wellbeing" — textbook wellness language, zero nativeness. Score: 2/10
- Cultural Match: The wellness coaching culture does not match the corporate professional culture being targeted. Score: 3/10
- Carrier Trust: Let us say moderate — some lived experience, decent consistency. Score: 14/25

RT = 4 × 2 × 3 × 14 = 336 (raw multiplicative; normalised in the composite layer scoring)

Even setting aside the mathematical complexity, the key takeaway from this scenario is:

**Pre-cap composite:** Even if RT, TU, and T all scored maximally, the total cannot exceed 40/100 because of the OV gate.

This practitioner could invest months in language optimisation, content mechanics, and identity work — and the total score could not break through the ceiling imposed by the non-viable offer. The diagnostic must direct the client's attention to offer structure before anything else.

### The Five Scoring Layers — Full Tables

**Layer 0: Offer Viability Gate — maximum 10 points**

| Raw OV Score (/20) | Layer 0 Score (/10) | Status |
|-------------------|---------------------|--------|
| 18-20 | 9-10 | Strong — gate passes cleanly |
| 14-17 | 7-8 | Adequate — gate passes with notes |
| 10-13 | 5-6 | Weak — gate passes but flag for development |
| Below 10 | Below 5 | GATE FAILS — total capped at 40/100 |

**Layer 1: Recognition Density — maximum 30 points**

| Sub-component | Maximum | Description |
|---------------|---------|-------------|
| Identity Stack Clarity | 10 | 2-3 identity layers densely stacked |
| Language Nativeness | 10 | All five language layers present and native |
| Cultural Safety | 10 | Full alignment with cultural rules of niche |

| L1 Total Score (/30) | Status |
|---------------------|--------|
| 24-30 | Elite recognition density. Ideal client stops immediately. |
| 18-23 | Strong foundation with specific gaps. |
| 10-17 | Moderate. Some right people stop, many pass. |
| Below 10 | Generic. Near-invisible to ideal client. L2 score HALVED. |

**Layer 2: Carrier Trust Signal Strength — maximum 25 points**

| Signal | Weight | Maximum Points |
|--------|--------|----------------|
| Lived Credibility | ×2 | 10 |
| Motive Transparency | ×1 | 2.5 |
| Constraint Alignment | ×1 | 2.5 |
| Consistency & Predictability | ×1 | 2.5 |
| Costly Signals | ×1 | 2.5 |
| Status Calibration | ×1 | 2.5 |
| Values Clarity | ×1 | 2.5 |
| **TOTAL** | | **25** |

| L2 Score (/25) | Status |
|----------------|--------|
| 20-25 | High carrier trust. Buyers feel safe following. |
| 15-19 | Moderate-high. Some visible gaps but broadly trustworthy. |
| 10-14 | Moderate. Conversion friction from trust gaps. |
| Below 10 | Low carrier trust. L3 + L4 scores HALVED. |

**Layer 3: Content Mechanics — maximum 20 points**

| Component | Maximum |
|-----------|---------|
| Hook quality | 4 |
| Pain frequency alignment | 4 |
| Magnetic polarity | 4 |
| Format execution | 4 |
| Window test | 4 |

**Layer 4: Conversion Architecture — maximum 15 points**

| Component | Maximum |
|-----------|---------|
| Profile-to-offer coherence | 3 |
| CTA clarity | 3 |
| DM readiness | 3 |
| Present test | 3 |
| Campaign infrastructure | 3 |

### Applying the Gate Rules

Gate 1: If L0 < 5/10 → Total score capped at 40/100 regardless of all other scores.

Gate 2: If L1 < 10/30 → L2 score is halved before summing. Rationale: if the positioning is invisible to the ideal client (recognition density failure), the carrier trust signals have no audience to reach. Trust cannot be built with people who never stopped to look.

Gate 3: If L2 (post-Gate 2 adjustment) < 10/25 → L3 and L4 scores are halved before summing. Rationale: content mechanics and conversion architecture cannot do their work if the person delivering the content is not trusted. Technically excellent content from an untrusted source does not convert.

**Score interpretation benchmarks:**

80-100: Elite niche execution. At this level, inbound leads are a natural byproduct of the positioning, not a result of effortful outreach. The niche functions as a self-reinforcing system.

60-79: Strong foundation with specific, addressable gaps. There is something working. The diagnostic identifies exactly what the single bottleneck is.

40-59: Structural niche problems. Content may create engagement or attention, but conversion is low because one or more of the fundamental layers is failing.

Below 40: Non-viable positioning or non-viable offer. Do not proceed to positioning refinement. Address the structural issue first.

---

## Section D: The Six Research Engines

### The Inside vs. Outside Distinction

Before describing the six engines, a critical principle: there is a hard distinction between research that can be done from outside the niche and research that requires being inside it.

Outside research — the kind the six engines perform — produces the external evidence: what communities exist, what competitors are doing, what language appears in public forums, what cultural rules can be inferred from visible behaviour. This research can be done quickly and accurately with the right tools and search discipline.

Inside research — the knowledge that can only be acquired through direct immersion — is about the things that never surface publicly: the exact words used in private conversations, the things people say to each other that they would never post, the cultural taboos that are so internalized that nobody discusses them explicitly, the private emotional experience of being in this world on a difficult day.

The loop between inside and outside research is the ongoing engine of positioning improvement: outside research produces a quick, accurate map of the visible landscape; inside research provides the texture and authenticity that turns that map into genuine cultural fluency; feeding inside knowledge back to the outside research improves every subsequent output.

The six engines are specifically outside research tools. They are tools for rapidly generating the raw inputs that the diagnostic mechanisms then process. Each engine is described below with a worked example showing realistic output for a specific niche: GP practice owners in Australia.

---

### Research Engine 1: Community Mapping

**What this engine does:** Identifies and maps every location where the target niche congregates — online and offline, active and passive.

**What it needs:**
- Niche description
- Any known platforms already identified
- Industry, geography, and subculture specifics

**Example output — GP practice owners in Australia:**

*Physical locations:*
- Primary Care Business Summit (annual, Sydney and Melbourne alternating) — the premier in-person event for practice owners, attendance typically 300-400
- RACGP (Royal Australian College of General Practitioners) annual conference — not specifically practice owners but high overlap
- State-based GP employer groups (e.g., Victorian GPNetwork, Queensland Primary Health Networks)
- Local PHN (Primary Health Network) advisory boards and workshops

*Online forums:*
- GP Down Under (Facebook group, 14,000+ members) — the dominant informal community for Australian GPs; practice management discussions appear regularly
- Practice Manager Hub Australia (Facebook group, 4,500 members) — practice managers, but owners participate; operational language is native here
- RACGP online CPD platform community discussions
- Reddit r/australia (medical professional threads) — lower quality but accessible

*Professional communities:*
- AGPAL (Australian General Practice Accreditation Limited) — all accredited practices interact here; compliance topics dominate
- MGMA equivalent: AAPM (Australian Association of Practice Management) — adjacent, practice manager-heavy but owner overlap significant

*Key newsletters:*
- Medical Republic (weekly briefing for primary care) — read by practice owners
- Australian Doctor (clinical + business mix)
- NewsGP (RACGP's newsletter, 30,000 subscribers, primarily clinicians including practice owners)

*Key podcasts:*
- The Medical Republic Podcast
- Panacea Health (practice business focus)

**How to use this output:** This map tells you exactly where to listen for language research, where to distribute content, and where to go for community access. Targeting the GP Down Under Facebook group with a specific search for "practice management" posts will surface raw, unfiltered language in 30 minutes of research.

---

### Research Engine 2: Competitor and Market Landscape

**Example output — GP practice owners in Australia:**

*Direct competitors (serving this niche specifically):*
- **HotDoc** — patient booking platform with practice management tools; heavily marketed to GP owners; positions around "patient experience" and "reducing admin burden"
- **Cubiko** — practice analytics platform; positions on "data-driven practice management"
- **Clinic Mastery** — coaching and consulting specifically for allied health and GP practice owners; positions on "building a business you're proud of"
- **Synapse Medical** — software and consulting for practice optimisation

*Adjacent competitors (serving a related need):*
- Major accounting firms with healthcare divisions (BDO Healthcare, Crowe Healthcare) — financial and business advisory for practice owners
- Healthcare-specific legal firms (TressCox Lawyers, Meridian Lawyers) — compliance and governance
- Healthcare recruiters — solving the practice owner's #1 operational problem (staffing)

*Dominant positioning angles already occupied:*
- "Software to reduce admin" — HotDoc, Best Practice, Medical Director
- "Financial compliance and tax optimisation" — accounting firms
- "More patients / better patient experience" — practice growth consultants
- "Accreditation and compliance" — AGPAL, training providers

*Gaps and differentiation opportunities:*
- GP practice owners who want to eventually sell their practice (exit planning) — underserved
- GP owners dealing with the specific transition from "I am the practice" to "I own the practice" — i.e., the de-centralisation of the owner from clinical work
- Practice owners struggling with the GP recruitment crisis in regional areas — highly active, inadequately addressed
- Financial literacy specifically for GP practice owners (different from accountant-mediated financial advice)

---

### Research Engine 3: Language and Vocabulary Research

**Example output — GP practice owners in Australia:**

*Functional phrases (what they do daily):*
- "Doing a session" (clinical work hours)
- "Service stream" or "bulk billing" / "mixed billing" / "private billing" (billing model)
- "Accreditation visit" (AGPAL review)
- "Chasing MBS items" (Medicare billing optimisation)
- "After-hours burden" (on-call obligations)
- "The front desk" (reception team, a constant source of operational friction)
- "Locum gaps" (unfilled session slots requiring locum doctors)
- "The principal" (a GP who is a practice principal — the ownership designation)
- "DR visits" (drug company representative visits — often financially significant)
- "Recall system" (patient follow-up management)

*Technical terms (insider vocabulary):*
- MBS (Medicare Benefits Schedule)
- PIP (Practice Incentives Programme — government funding)
- QI PIP (Quality Improvement Practice Incentives Programme)
- ePIP (eHealth Practice Incentives Programme)
- AHPRA (practitioner registration body)
- RACGP Standards (the accreditation framework)
- Item numbers (MBS billing codes)
- CDM (Chronic Disease Management — a billing pathway)
- TCA (Team Care Arrangement — a CDM billing component)
- HbA1c (clinical metric that practice owners track for CDM population management)

*Psychic phrases (what they feel but rarely say:)*
- "I'm basically a landlord with a medical degree" (the feeling of being economically dependent on GP tenant-contractors rather than truly running a business)
- "I can't take a holiday without the whole thing falling apart"
- "I didn't do 10 years of training to spend half my time doing payroll"
- "The Medicare system hasn't changed but our costs have doubled"
- "I own the place but I have less control than the tenant GPs"

*Narrative arcs (recurring story patterns):*
1. The Reluctant Business Owner: became a GP to practise medicine, gradually acquired/inherited the practice, now feels overwhelmed by the business complexity that was never in the plan
2. The Scaling Plateau: expanded from one site to two or three, and now the complexity is growing faster than the revenue
3. The Exit Planning Arc: approaching 55-65, starting to think about what the practice is worth and how to transition out without abandoning patients

---

### Research Engine 4: Pain Point and Frustration Research

**Example output — GP practice owners in Australia:**

*Daily pains (Gold):*
- Roster gaps — every day, the practice principal is checking whether sessions are covered. A single GP not coming in can cost $3,000-$8,000 in revenue for the day
- Medicare billing complexity — missed item numbers, rejected claims, staff who don't know the correct billing pathways
- Staff management friction — the front desk is consistently the most complained-about operational element
- Contractor management — GP tenant relationships require delicate management; too much pressure on productivity creates attrition, too little loses revenue

*Weekly pains:*
- Payroll and rostering cycle management
- Performance of the recall system (were patients followed up?)
- Unpaid accounts and private billing collections
- CPD and accreditation tracking for employed staff

*Monthly pains:*
- P&L review — most practice owners do not look at their numbers frequently enough to catch problems early
- PIP and incentive programme reporting
- Lease and building maintenance decisions
- Review of MBS billing analysis reports

*Anchor moment:*
The anchor moment for GP practice owners is **Monday morning at 7:30am** — when they arrive to find which GPs have called in sick, whether the front desk has any cover problems, and whether any urgent compliance issues came through over the weekend. This is the highest-frequency concentration point for daily pain. Content that opens with "Monday morning at the practice" is the highest-relevance hook for this niche.

---

### Research Engine 5: Cultural Norms and Buying Behaviour

**Example output — GP practice owners in Australia:**

*Credibility rules:*
- Clinical credentials matter for basic trust but do not differentiate. Every practice owner is a doctor.
- What earns trust is demonstrated familiarity with the operational specifics — knowing what an MBS item number is, understanding the difference between bulk billing and mixed billing and the economics of each, understanding the contractor-vs-employee distinction and its legal implications.
- Peer referral is the primary trust mechanism. A recommendation from another GP practice principal carries disproportionate weight.
- Case studies involving practices of similar size (number of consulting rooms, billing model, geography) are significantly more credible than general testimonials.

*Status rules:*
- "Multi-site practice owner" is high status.
- "Fully private billing" is high status (signals a premium patient base and a managed business rather than a Medicare-dependent one).
- Being heavily reliant on locums is low status (signals poor planning or poor culture).
- External business credentials (MBA, management qualifications) are generally neutral to slightly positive — unlike in some clinical communities where they are slightly suspicious.

*Taboo inventory:*
- Claiming to know more about clinical practice than the doctors themselves.
- Implying that GPs are primarily motivated by money (even if true; it is not said directly).
- Hard-selling without establishing peer-level respect first. Cold pitching a GP practice owner is often immediately rejected unless the opening demonstrates genuine familiarity with the specific operational world.
- Generic healthcare consulting positioning that doesn't distinguish between GP and hospital contexts.

*Tempo profile:*
- Medium pace. Not the immediate-response startup culture; not the ultra-formal legal pace.
- Content depth is respected — GPs are educated, analytical people who appreciate substance.
- Trust is built over 3-6 months of consistent value delivery before most purchase decisions are made.
- Short-form content is consumed but decisions happen after longer engagement.

*Buying behaviour:*
- Word of mouth within GP networks is the primary discovery mechanism.
- Discovery is followed by a research phase of weeks to months.
- Trial of tools/platforms before full commitment (especially software).
- Peer validation sought before final purchase of high-ticket consulting services.
- Price sensitivity in the $5,000-$25,000 annual range — practice owners are cost-conscious because their margins are thin and highly variable.

---

### Research Engine 6: Market Data and Congregation Points

**Example output — GP practice owners in Australia:**

*Market data:*
- Approximately 6,700 GP practices in Australia (RACGP data).
- Average practice has 3-8 consulting GPs (mix of principals and contractor GPs).
- GP practice revenue ranges from approximately $600,000 to $8M+ depending on size, billing model, and location.
- The market is under significant pressure: GP recruitment crisis (especially regional), MBS indexation not keeping pace with costs, increasing competition from corporatised GP chains (Sonic, Healthscope, etc.).
- Market direction: consolidation (solo and small practices being acquired by larger corporate groups) and slow but accelerating shift toward mixed/private billing models.
- Typical spend on business consulting/management services: $10,000-$60,000 per year for medium to large practices. Smaller practices under-spend significantly on this.

*Key market timing signals:*
- RACGP Standards for General Practice (5th Edition) release — creates acute accreditation-related demand.
- Federal Budget healthcare announcements — create both anxiety (funding changes) and buying readiness (planning for change).
- Medicare Benefits Schedule Reviews — drives billing compliance anxiety.

*Congregation point map:*
- GP Down Under (Facebook): primary online community, 14,000 members, active daily
- RACGP Annual Conference: 2,000+ attendees, held October/November
- Practice Management Summit (HotDoc/various): ~400 attendees
- State PHN advisory groups: regional, high-trust, invitation-based

---

## Section E: The Full Step-by-Step Scoring Process

### Overview of the Five Phases

The diagnostic runs across five phases. Each phase has specific inputs, outputs, and gates. A practitioner who shortcuts any phase will produce an incomplete diagnostic that misses the structural issue and generates recommendations that address symptoms rather than causes.

---

### Phase 0: Niche Intake and Initial Scoping

**Step 0.1 — Collect the niche description.**

Ask: "Describe your niche. Who do you help, what do you help them with, and what do you know specifically about the people you help?"

You are listening for three things: a who (specific enough to be an identity, not a demographic), a what (a problem or transformation, not a capability), and a where (a congregation point, even if implied).

**Step 0.2 — Push for specificity if needed.**

If the answer is "I help entrepreneurs grow their business," you do not yet have enough to proceed. Use the room prompt: "If I walked into a room full of your ten best-fit clients — what would I notice? What would they be wearing, complaining about, worried about? What's on their phone?" This question breaks through abstraction by grounding the niche in sensory and situational specifics.

**Gate 0:** Do not proceed to research until the description contains at least one subculture identity, one specific pain point, and one plausible congregation point. Without these, the research engines have nothing to aim at and will produce generic outputs that cannot be scored against.

---

### Phase 1: Outside Research (Painting the House)

**Step 1.1 — Run the six research engines.**

Execute all six research engines in parallel. Use web search in deep research mode. Run a minimum of five to ten targeted searches per engine. The standard is: name actual communities with actual URLs, name actual competitors with actual positioning, produce actual language bank entries from actual forum posts. Generic outputs ("there are Facebook groups for this niche") are not adequate outputs.

**Step 1.2 — Run preliminary scoring.**

Before looking at the client's existing assets, score the five primary engines against the research data:
- OV: based on market evidence from Engines 2 and 6
- Identity Match: based on the specificity of the niche description
- Language Match: based on the language bank from Engine 3
- Cultural Match: based on the cultural norms from Engine 5
- Carrier Trust: based on whatever the client has shared

This preliminary scoring identifies the theoretical ceiling — the best these engines could score given an ideal execution of this niche. It also flags immediately if the offer viability gate will trigger before any content is produced.

**Step 1.3 — Generate the outside assets.**

From the research, produce these specific deliverables:
- Language Bank: 10 functional phrases, 10 technical terms, 5 psychic phrases, 3 narrative arcs
- 5 hook examples using the Movie Trailer engine
- 3 DM opener examples using the 3-Part Gift Rule
- Pain Frequency Map: daily/weekly/monthly pains + anchor moment
- Community and distribution map with specific locations and access routes

**Gate 1 — Identify the five inside gaps.**

Before closing Phase 1, generate the five questions that cannot be answered from outside research:
1. Daily emotional experience question: "What is the single worst moment of your typical work week, emotionally?"
2. Cultural taboo question: "What do you never say to your clients, even though you think it regularly?"
3. Buying trigger question: "What was the specific event that made your best clients finally decide to work with someone like you, rather than just researching it?"
4. Language nuance question: "When you describe your work to a peer at a conference, without trying to pitch — what words do you use?"
5. Identity tension question: "Is there any part of the identity your niche presents publicly that conflicts with who they actually feel like they are?"

These questions must be answered through real conversation, and they generate the inside intelligence that separates native-language positioning from researched-language positioning.

---

### Phase 2: Inside Diagnostic (Full Asset Review)

**Step 2.1 — Collect client assets.**

Request: LinkedIn profile URL or full text of headline, About section, and Experience section. Five to ten recent LinkedIn posts (or equivalent content from their primary platform). Website or landing page URL. Optionally: an interview transcript, a voice note, or video content. Answers to the five Phase 1 inside gap questions.

**Step 2.2 — Run Engine 1: Offer Viability Gate. Always first.**

Score OV = D + C + O + P. Do not proceed to content analysis until you know whether the gate triggers. If OV < 5/10, the diagnostic still runs — the client still needs to understand what is broken — but the recommendations must prioritise offer structure above all positioning work. State this explicitly and immediately: "The offer viability gate has triggered. Here is what the data shows about the gap and what needs to be addressed before positioning work will move the needle."

**Step 2.3 — Run Engines 2-4: Recognition Layer (Layer 1).**

Work through each profile element and content piece systematically:

For Identity Match (Engine 2): Read the headline and About section. How many identity layers are present? Score each one on 0-5. Identify which layers are absent.

For Language Match (Engine 3): Take three pieces of content. Apply the noun removal test to each. Identify which language layers are present and which are absent. Note specific phrases that are native and specific phrases that are generic.

For Cultural Match (Engine 4): Compare the tone, credibility signals, and status language in the content against the cultural rules map from Engine 5 in the research phase. Identify specific misalignments.

**Gate 2:** Sum the Layer 1 scores. If the total is below 10/30, note that the Layer 2 score will be halved in the composite.

**Step 2.4 — Run Engine 5: Carrier Trust (Layer 2).**

Review all assets for evidence of the seven carrier trust signals. For Lived Credibility, the diagnostic question is: "Can I find, in this content, specific references to having been inside this world that could not have been manufactured from outside?" Note the answer specifically — it is the most important single signal in the system.

**Gate 3:** If Layer 2 total will be below 10/25, note that Layers 3 and 4 will be halved.

**Step 2.5 — Run Engines 6-8: Content Mechanics and Conversion (Layers 3-4).**

Review content against the four Window Tests and the five Virality Dimensions. Review the profile and website for conversion architecture. Score each component.

**Step 2.6 — Run supporting engines as applicable.**

Pain Frequency Map: does the content address daily pains or only monthly ones? Magnetic Polarity: does the content attract and repel, or is it trying to appeal to everyone? Hook quality: score the opening lines of the last five posts.

---

### Phase 3: Composite Scoring

**Step 3.1 — Assemble the layer scores.**

Enter all raw scores into the layer table:

| Layer | Engine(s) | Raw Score | Max |
|-------|-----------|-----------|-----|
| L0: Offer Viability | OV = D+C+O+P | | /10 |
| L1: Recognition Density | Identity + Language + Cultural | | /30 |
| L2: Carrier Trust | 7 signals | | /25 |
| L3: Content Mechanics | Content + Trust Utility | | /20 |
| L4: Conversion Architecture | Timing + Pressure + CTA | | /15 |

**Step 3.2 — Apply gate rules in sequence.**

First: Is L0 below 5? If yes, note the cap. Still score everything else — the client needs the full picture.
Second: Is L1 below 10? If yes, halve the L2 score before proceeding.
Third: Is L2 (post-Gate 2) below 10? If yes, halve L3 + L4 before summing.

**Step 3.3 — Compute the total and identify the bottleneck.**

The bottleneck is the layer with the largest absolute gap from its maximum after applying gate corrections. This is the single most important output of the diagnostic because it identifies where to invest first.

---

### Phase 4: Report Generation

Write the full diagnostic report in the structure described in Section F below. The diagnostic should feel like a coach who studied everything about the subject — it should reference exact lines from their content, exact sections of their profile, exact language choices. Generic diagnostic reports ("your language could be more specific") are not adequate. The report must be specific enough that the subject knows immediately which line, which post, and which choice is being diagnosed.

---

### Phase 5: Re-Score After Implementation

After the client implements the recommendations, re-collect assets, re-run all engines, re-apply gate rules, and produce an updated score. The before/after comparison table is the single most compelling evidence that the diagnostic framework is working. It also identifies new bottlenecks — the second-order constraints that only become visible after the first-order ones are resolved.

---

## Section F: Example Scored Output — Complete Fictional Diagnostic Report

### Subject: Sarah Chen — Business Exit Consultant for Female Founders in Tech

**Context:** Sarah Chen spent twelve years building and eventually selling a SaaS company in the HR tech space. Post-exit, she took six months to decompress before realising that the most valuable thing she could offer was helping other female founders in tech navigate the exit process — specifically the psychological, strategic, and negotiation components that most M&A advisers do not address.

She has been posting on LinkedIn for eight months. She has a website. She has had fifteen discovery calls from inbound leads but converted only two. She believes her positioning is the problem.

---

### Executive Summary

Sarah Chen has genuine, differentiated expertise and strong carrier trust signals — the foundational elements are in place. The diagnostic reveals two specific structural failures that are suppressing conversion despite adequate attention. First, her identity stack uses a single layer (female tech founders) where it should use three, resulting in content that attracts a wide audience but converts a narrow one. Second, her content is consistently excellent on reflection but systematically fails the Step-Through test — readers feel seen and engaged but are given no clear path to acting on that engagement.
