# Section 9: Science Appendix — The Academic Foundations of TSE V2

**Section:** 9 of 10
**Target word count:** 10,000 words
**Status:** Assembled from raw/08_science.md (primary) and raw/14_research_synthesis.md
**Date:** 2026-03-25

---

## How to Read This Appendix

The Tribal Scoring Engine V2 is not a framework invented from first principles. It is a scoring instrument assembled from 12 academic disciplines, deduplicated into 25 master mechanisms, and then subjected to a systematic evidence audit covering 101 of 123 total mechanisms.

This appendix exists for two purposes:

**Purpose 1 — Epistemic honesty.** TSE V2 makes precise quantitative claims: that CSS-2 contamination applies a 0.75 multiplier, that MM-10B scores at 50 for partial synchrony, that identity fusion creates price insensitivity at TP 700–800. These are not assertions made to sound authoritative. Each has a derivation path traceable to peer-reviewed literature. This appendix documents those paths.

**Purpose 2 — Appropriate confidence calibration.** Not all mechanisms are equally well-grounded. Some values are directly derivable from meta-analytic data (MM-10B Score 50 is one of those). Others are working estimates extrapolated from adjacent literature. A practitioner scoring a real niche deserves to know which is which.

Every claim in this appendix carries an evidence classification. The direction of most effects is well-supported. The magnitudes remain uncertain for B2B creator contexts specifically. Read the confidence ratings before treating any point estimate as exact.

---

## Part 1: The Evidence Classification System

### The Five Evidence Levels — Full Definitions and Counts

Phase 4 of the TSE V2 rebuild established a five-level classification system applied to every scored mechanism. These levels were applied consistently across all 101 reviewed mechanisms and are the primary transparency tool for practitioners using this system.

---

**Level 1 — KEEP_WITH_BOUNDS (55 mechanisms)**

Definition: The mechanism's value is supported by peer-reviewed literature. The direction of the effect is confirmed across multiple studies. The magnitude is within the empirically defensible range, though not precisely pinned to a single point estimate. The value is retained but is now reported with an explicit confidence interval.

What "KEEP_WITH_BOUNDS" means in practice: Use the value. Report the range. Do not treat the point estimate as exact. These 55 mechanisms are the backbone of the scoring system and represent the strongest evidence base.

Example application: MM-10B Score 50 ("partial synchrony, distributed participation") carries KEEP_WITH_BOUNDS status. Two independent meta-analyses — Rennung & Goritz (2016, k=31 synchrony studies) and Mogan et al. (2017, k=42 synchrony studies) — directly confirm that partial synchrony produces roughly 50–70% of full-synchrony prosocial effects, making the 50/100 scoring ratio defensible within ±10 points.

---

**Level 2 — REPLACE_WITH_RANGE (17 mechanisms)**

Definition: The current value was outside the evidence midpoint — either too high or too low relative to what the literature actually supports. The value has been replaced with a better-grounded estimate, and a confidence interval is now provided.

What "REPLACE_WITH_RANGE" means in practice: Use the new (replaced) value. Discard the old value entirely. The replacement is not a guess — it is derived from the same literature that revealed the original was off-center.

Example application: The TSSM long-window modifier was +0.25 in the original proposal. Phase 4 analysis of Jablin (2001) and Ng & Feldman (2007) — covering 200+ and 80+ longitudinal studies respectively — showed the actual purchase-activation effect for 5+ year cohort transitions is small (d~0.10–0.20). The value was replaced with +0.12, range [+0.05–+0.20]. Using the old +0.25 would overstate projected urgency by approximately 2×.

A second high-priority replacement: The BAC 2–3 violation multiplier was ×0.40. Slovic (1993) asymmetry ratio (negative events weighted 2.5–3× more than positive) and Kim et al. (2004) showing ~50% drop in forgiveness probability after second violation both support a lower penalty. Replaced with ×0.35, range [0.25–0.50].

---

**Level 3 — FLAG_AS_HYPOTHESIS (27 mechanisms)**

Definition: The direction of the effect is supported by adjacent or related literature, but the specific magnitude — and in some cases the specific operationalization — has not been tested in B2B creator contexts. The value is a working estimate, not a derived value.

What "FLAG_AS_HYPOTHESIS" means in practice: Use the mechanism with explicit uncertainty labels. Do not present as established fact in client-facing materials. These values require empirical calibration against real scoring data before they can be treated as reliable point estimates. The direction is sound; the exact numbers are not.

Example application: The MM-05_composite Sacred Values / IBRD weighting (60%/40%) is FLAG_AS_HYPOTHESIS. Graham et al. (2009) Moral Foundations Theory shows explicit moral foundations explain only 40–60% of group identity variance. Nisbett & Cohen (1996) honor culture data suggests implicit norms may be 4–5× stronger than explicit values in high-IBRD niches. The 60/40 split is defensible but the correct split in high-IBRD niches may be closer to 50/50. No direct study has tested this in a B2B creator scoring context.

---

**Level 4 — NO_SCIENCE_EXISTS (2 mechanisms)**

Definition: No empirical basis exists for the proposed value. Neither the mechanism's magnitude nor its formula placement can be anchored to any peer-reviewed evidence. These values are pure hypotheses with no adjacent literature support.

What "NO_SCIENCE_EXISTS" means in practice: Do NOT use in production scoring. Restrict to EXPLORATORY module only, with explicit labeling. Both mechanisms in this category are IRS formula weights — specifically the ORTD weight (0.15) and the BAD weight (0.20) in the modified IRS formula.

Critical disclosure: "Both weights (0.20 BAD weight and 0.15 ORTD weight) have NO empirical basis. No derivation exists for the 0.20 value or the 0.20/0.15 BAD/ORTD ratio." These weights require prospective empirical calibration against 50+ scored niches with tracked conversion outcomes before any production use is appropriate.

---

**Level 5 — NOT_YET_REVIEWED (22 mechanisms)**

Definition: These mechanisms were not covered in the Phase 4 literature audit. They carry the original proposed values from Phase 1 and Phase 3 research, which were working estimates derived from adjacent literature rather than systematic evidence review.

What "NOT_YET_REVIEWED" means in practice: Treat as provisional estimates. These are not uncalibrated in the same way as Level 4 — they were derived from real research, not invented. But they have not been subjected to the same systematic scrutiny as the 101 reviewed mechanisms.

Mechanism categories in this group include: several Tier 3 sub-scores (MM-16 through MM-25 rubric details), some Tier 2 sub-scores within MM-06 through MM-09, and the UAS (Urgency Activation Score) which is explicitly EXPLORATORY.

---

### Evidence Coverage Summary Table

| Classification | Count | Use in Production |
|----------------|-------|-------------------|
| KEEP_WITH_BOUNDS | 55 | Yes — with confidence interval |
| REPLACE_WITH_RANGE | 17 | Yes — use new value only |
| FLAG_AS_HYPOTHESIS | 27 | With explicit uncertainty labels |
| NO_SCIENCE_EXISTS | 2 | No — EXPLORATORY only |
| NOT_YET_REVIEWED | 22 | Provisional estimates only |
| **Total** | **123** | |

**Reviewed against literature:** 101 of 123 mechanisms (82.1%)

The 22 not-yet-reviewed mechanisms are targeted for Phase 6 coverage before any production scoring tool is finalized.

## Part 2: The 12 Academic Disciplines

TSE V2 was built from 12 primary academic disciplines, each contributing specific mechanisms. The science-grounded rebuild in Phase 5 drew on peer-reviewed evidence from all 12. This section documents each discipline: what it contributes to TSE V2, the key researchers and papers, and which specific mechanisms it supports.

---

### Discipline 1: Evolutionary Psychology and Coalition Psychology

**Core insight:** Humans evolved as coalition members, not solo agents. For 99% of human history, survival depended on belonging to a group of 50–150 people who could recognize you, vouch for you, and fight alongside you. This ancient coalition-recognition circuitry still fires in modern contexts — it determines whether a prospect pre-rationally trusts a consultant before reading a single word of copy.

The evolutionary framework explains several features of tribal behavior that have no other parsimonious explanation: why in-group trust fires in under 170ms (Otten & Moskowitz 2000), why out-group hostility is automatic rather than deliberate (Tajfel & Turner 1979), and why group size follows predictable scaling laws (Dunbar 1992). These are not cultural artifacts — they are hardware.

**Key researchers:** Nettle & Dunbar (1997), Barth (1969), Otten & Moskowitz (2000), Henrich & Gil-White (2001), Kurzban & Leary (2001), Sherif (1954), Hamilton (1964), Silk (2003)

**Mechanisms supported:**
- MM-01 (Tribal Vocabulary Exclusivity): Kinship marker recognition — in-group signals processed in under 170ms before rational evaluation
- MM-02 (Entitativity / Tribe Realness): The "real group" perception threshold
- MM-03 (Shared Enemy Clarity): Coalitional outgroup hostility — in-group cohesion strengthened by outgroup definition
- MM-04 (Initiation Structure): Handicap principle applied to group entry costs
- MM-07 (Status Hierarchy Clarity): Prestige vs. dominance hierarchy tracking — Henrich & Gil-White (2001) distinguish the two
- MM-13 (Common Fate / Existential Binding): Shared fate activating collective threat response
- MM-16 (Optimal Group Size): Dunbar numbers establishing the tribal viability window

**Key published papers:**
- Zahavi, A. (1975). Mate selection — a selection for a handicap. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*, 53, 205–214.
- Henrich, J., & Gil-White, F. (2001). The evolution of prestige. *Evolution and Human Behavior*, 22, 165–196.
- Nettle, D., & Dunbar, R. (1997). Social markers and the evolution of reciprocal exchange. *Current Anthropology*, 38, 93–99.
- Otten, S., & Moskowitz, G. (2000). Evidence for implicit evaluative in-group bias. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*, 36, 481–497.
- Dunbar, R.I.M. (1992). Neocortex size as a constraint on group size in primates. *Journal of Human Evolution*, 22, 469–493.

---

### Discipline 2: Social Identity Theory (SIT) and Self-Categorization Theory (SCT)

**Core insight:** People derive significant portions of their self-concept from the social groups they belong to. When a social category becomes salient, people adopt the prototype of that category as their own identity, feel interchangeable with in-group members, and see out-group members as fundamentally different. This is the mechanism by which purchases become acts of identity affirmation rather than utility maximization.

SIT (Tajfel & Turner 1979) and SCT (Turner et al. 1987) form the theoretical spine of TSE V2. The three-tier mechanism structure, the weighted score formula, and the TP-to-conversion relationship all rest on the prediction that identity salience is the primary driver of tribal purchasing behavior. Reed et al. (2012) meta-analysis (d=0.54, k=23) provides the strongest aggregate confirmation: identity-congruent marketing substantially outperforms identity-neutral approaches.

**Key researchers:** Tajfel & Turner (1979, 1986), Turner et al. (1987), Stryker (1980), Hogg & Terry (2000), Forehand & Deshpandé (2001), Escalas & Bettman (2003, 2005), Reed (2002, 2012), Oyserman (2009)

**Mechanisms supported:**
- MM-02 (Entitativity): Directly — entitativity is an SIT construct
- MM-03 (Shared Enemy Clarity): Outgroup differentiation as identity sharpening
- MM-06 (Identity Fusion Depth): SIT extended to the fusion state (personal-group boundary dissolution)
- MM-07 (Status Hierarchy Clarity): Prestige hierarchy as social comparison mechanism
- MM-25 (Prototype Clarity): SCT prototype matching directly grounds this mechanism
- T1 tier 60% weight: Stryker (1980) identity salience hierarchy establishes gateway mechanisms as primary activation threshold

**Key published papers:**
- Tajfel, H., & Turner, J.C. (1979). An integrative theory of intergroup conflict. *In The Social Psychology of Intergroup Relations*, 33–47.
- Turner, J.C., et al. (1987). *Rediscovering the Social Group: A Self-Categorization Theory*. Oxford: Blackwell.
- Stryker, S. (1980). *Symbolic Interactionism: A Social Structural Version*. Menlo Park: Benjamin/Cummings.
- Reed, A., et al. (2012). Identity-based consumer behavior. *International Journal of Research in Marketing*, 29, 310–321. (d=0.54, k=23)
- Lickel, B., et al. (2000). Varieties of groups and the perception of group entitativity. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*, 78, 223–246.
- Escalas, J.E., & Bettman, J.R. (2003). You are what they eat. *Journal of Consumer Psychology*, 13, 339–348. (r=0.31 vs. r=0.62 — the 2× tribal-brand gap)

---

### Discipline 3: Costly Signaling Theory and Identity Fusion Theory

**Core insight (Costly Signaling Theory):** Credible signals of group membership must be expensive enough to be unfakeable. Zahavi's 1975 Handicap Principle established that reliable signals must impose genuine costs on the signaler — costs that cheaters cannot pay without being detected. Applied to human groups: communities demanding expensive entry rituals develop stronger cooperation, higher trust, and more durable loyalty than low-cost communities. The cost is the proof.

**Core insight (Identity Fusion Theory):** Identity fusion describes the literal psychological merger of personal self-concept with group identity. Fused individuals are not just loyal — they experience the group's outcomes as their own and will sacrifice disproportionate personal resources to protect the group. This is the mechanism behind price insensitivity at high TP scores: purchasing from a same-tribe consultant is not a transaction, it is self-defense.

**Key researchers:** Zahavi (1975, 1977), Sosis & Alcorta (2003), Sosis & Bressler (2003), Swann et al. (2009, 2012), Swann & Buhrmester (2015), Bliege Bird & Smith (2005)

**Mechanisms supported:**
- MM-04 (Initiation Structure): Costly signaling as entry credential
- MM-06 (Identity Fusion Depth): IFT directly grounds this mechanism — the VFS threshold at TP 700–800
- MM-20 (Economic Lock-In): Irreversible investment as commitment signal
- MM-21 (Linguistic Register Depth): Humor weight elevated because schema-gated humor is structurally unfakeable
- IFS rubric: "Tribal brand anchors with exclusivity knowledge" (+8 pts) grounded in Bliege Bird & Smith (2005)

**Key published papers:**
- Zahavi, A. (1975). Mate selection — a selection for a handicap. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*, 53, 205–214.
- Sosis, R., & Bressler, E. (2003). Cooperation and commune longevity. *Cross-Cultural Research*, 37, 211–239.
- Swann, W.B., et al. (2009). Identity fusion: The interplay of personal and social identities. *Psychological Bulletin*, 135, 751–765.
- Swann, W.B., et al. (2012). When group membership gets personal. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*, 103, 1086–1101.
- Bliege Bird, R., & Smith, E.A. (2005). Signaling theory, strategic interaction, and symbolic capital. *Current Anthropology*, 46, 221–248.

---

### Discipline 4: Embodied Cognition

**Core insight:** Cognition is not purely abstract — it is grounded in the body's sensorimotor experience. Text that evokes embodied states (physical sensations, temperature, smell, muscle memory) bypasses the semantic processing layer and enters episodic memory directly. This is the mechanism behind the IFS rubric's highest-value content signals.

Petrova & Cialdini (2005) demonstrated 18–32% purchase intent increase when offers were accompanied by sensory-rich imagery. Sadoski & Paivio's dual-coding meta-analysis (~80 studies, d=0.54) confirmed embodied encoding produces superior memory and preference formation. These are not aesthetic claims — they are neurological predictions.

**Key researchers:** Niedenthal et al. (2005), Glenberg & Kaschak (2002), Zhong & Leonardelli (2008), Sadoski & Paivio (2001), Petrova & Cialdini (2005), Lakoff & Johnson (1999)

**Mechanisms supported:**
- IFS rubric: "Body state or physical sensation described" (+12 points, range [8–16])
- IFS rubric: "Level 4 sensory world paragraph" (+12 points, range [9–16])
- MM-14 (Physical Territory): Somatic place identity — the pre-verbal recognition of territory fires at 100–200ms

**Key published papers:**
- Niedenthal, P.M., et al. (2005). Embodiment in attitudes, social perception, and emotion. *Personality and Social Psychology Review*, 9, 184–211. (d=0.38–0.62)
- Glenberg, A.M., & Kaschak, M.P. (2002). Grounding language in action. *Psychonomic Bulletin & Review*, 9, 558–565. (d=0.54)
- Sadoski, M., & Paivio, A. (2001). *Imagery and Text: A Dual Coding Theory of Reading and Writing*. Mahwah: Erlbaum.
- Petrova, P.K., & Cialdini, R.B. (2005). Fluency of consumption imagery and the backfire effects of imagery appeals. *Journal of Consumer Research*, 32, 442–452. (18–32% purchase intent increase)

---

### Discipline 5: Ritual Studies

**Core insight:** Rituals are not mere ceremonies. They are synchrony technologies — behavioral mechanisms that create neurological bonding through shared, structured, costly, or emotionally intense collective action. Whitehouse's two-pathway model distinguishes low-arousal/high-frequency rituals (daily prayers, regular workouts) from high-arousal/low-frequency rituals (initiation ceremonies, extreme events). Both produce identity consolidation but through different mechanisms.

The practical consequence for tribal scoring: a niche with a strong ritual calendar has built-in collective memory. Members who all did "the CrossFit Open" in the same week share a structural memory that functions as a tribal recognition device. A consultant who references the Open correctly accesses that memory — and the bonding it encoded.

**Key researchers:** Whitehouse & Lanman (2014), Turner, V. (1969), Páez et al. (2015), Wiltermuth & Heath (2009), Rennung & Goritz (2016), Mogan et al. (2017), Collins, R. (2004)

**Mechanisms supported:**
- MM-08 (Synchronized Ritual & Calendar): Primary mechanism — full synchrony grounds the 75 and 100 scores
- MM-10B (CET Density): Partial synchrony grounds the ANCHOR 50 score — the most directly supported value in the system
- MM-11 (Shared Suffering): Communitas theory (Turner 1969) directly grounds this mechanism
- IFS rubric: "Temporal ritual with precision" (+8 points, grounded in Whitehouse & Lanman 2014 d=0.21)

**Key published papers:**
- Whitehouse, H., & Lanman, J.A. (2014). The ties that bind us. *Current Anthropology*, 55, 674–695.
- Rennung, M., & Goritz, A.S. (2016). Prosocial consequences of interpersonal synchrony. *Zeitschrift für Psychologie*, 224, 168–189. (k=31 synchrony studies)
- Mogan, R., Fischer, R., & Bulbulia, J.A. (2017). To be in synchrony or not? *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*, 72, 39–49. (k=42 synchrony studies)
- Wiltermuth, S.S., & Heath, C. (2009). Synchrony and cooperation. *Psychological Science*, 20, 1–5.
- Bastian, B., et al. (2014). Pain as social glue. *Psychological Science*, 25, 2079–2085. (d=0.6–0.8)

---

### Discipline 6: Collective Memory

**Core insight:** Groups maintain shared memories that function differently from individual memories — they are more durable, more emotionally charged, and more identity-constitutive. Flashbulb memories (dramatic shared events) produce r=0.61 collective encoding strength vs. r=0.31 for individual events (Luminet & Curci 2009). This difference is the mechanism behind IFS Level 5A content (structural collective memory) outperforming Level 5B content (unique personal biography).

A key distinction: structural collective memory (the entire tribe experienced this together) vs. unique biographical memory (only the creator experienced this). Content deploying structural collective memory creates recognition in every reader who belongs to the tribe. Content deploying unique biographical memory creates recognition in no one.

**Key researchers:** Halbwachs, M. (1992), Luminet & Curci (2009), Talarico & Rubin (2003), Hirst et al. (2018), Neisser, U. (1982)

**Mechanisms supported:**
- MM-09 (Collective Memory & Sacred Narrative): Direct — Halbwachs is the foundational theorist
- MM-10B (CET Density): Shared temporal events are operationalized collective memories
- IFS rubric: "Collective timestamp from personal memory" (+10 points, range [8–12])

**Key published papers:**
- Halbwachs, M. (1992). *On Collective Memory*. Chicago: University of Chicago Press.
- Luminet, O., & Curci, A. (Eds.). (2009). *Flashbulb Memories: New Issues and New Perspectives*. New York: Psychology Press. (9-country synthesis, r=0.61 vs. r=0.31)
- Hirst, W., et al. (2018). A ten-year follow-up of a study of memory for the attack of September 11, 2001. *Journal of Experimental Psychology: General*, 147, 209–228. (d=0.45–0.65)

---

### Discipline 7: Linguistic Anthropology

**Core insight:** Language is not just communication — it is a tribal membership credential. The way a community uses language (register, humor, complaint patterns) is both a signal of membership and a mechanism of bonding. The crucial finding for TSE V2: linguistic register has a three-tier imitation-resistance hierarchy that was previously inverted in V1 scoring.

The rebuild corrected the hierarchy. Vocabulary (lexical register) is the most learnable and most imitable signal — it should carry less weight, not more. Complaint patterns (pragmatic register) and insider humor (humor register) are the least imitable and should carry higher weight. The previous V1 weights (Lexical 40% / Pragmatic 30% / Humor 30%) were replaced with (Lexical 30% / Pragmatic 35% / Humor 35%).

**Key researchers:** Shepard et al. (2001), Lehman et al. (2019), Oring, E. (1992), Dunbar et al. (2012), Bosson et al. (2006), Halliday, M.A.K. (1978)

**Mechanisms supported:**
- MM-01 (Tribal Vocabulary Exclusivity): Communication Accommodation Theory grounds the vocabulary-as-membership-marker mechanism
- MM-21 (Linguistic Register Depth): Three-tier rebuilt weights (30/35/35)
- Linguistic Engine (5 language layers): Functional, Technical, Cultural, Psychic, Narrative

**Key published papers:**
- Dunbar, R.I.M., et al. (2012). Social laughter is correlated with an elevated pain threshold. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B*, 279, 1161–1167. (d=0.82 — strongest bonding effect in the domain)
- Bosson, J.K., et al. (2006). Interpersonal chemistry through negativity. *Personal Relationships*, 13, 135–150. (d=0.68–0.74)
- Oring, E. (1992). *Jokes and their Relations*. Lexington: University Press of Kentucky. (schema-gated humor theory)
- Shepard, C.A., et al. (2001). Communication accommodation theory. *New Handbook of Language and Social Psychology*, 33–56. (r=0.40–0.55, k=40–60)

---

### Discipline 8: Brand Tribalism and Material Culture

**Core insight:** Branded objects and material artifacts are not just products — they are tribal identity markers that sort members from non-members and encode membership knowledge. Brand literacy functions as a tribal credential. The critical empirical finding: Escalas & Bettman (2003) measured r=0.31 for surface brand connection vs. r=0.62 for tribal-brand connection — a 2× difference. These are not the same construct at different intensities; they are categorically different states.

This 2× gap justifies the nonlinear BAD multiplier structure in TSE V2 (0.60 → 0.75 → 0.88 → 1.00 → 1.10). You cannot linearly interpolate between superficial brand familiarity and genuine tribal brand command. The jump happens at a categorical threshold, not along a gradient.

**Key researchers:** Muniz & O'Guinn (2001), Schouten & McAlexander (1995), Schau et al. (2009), Escalas & Bettman (2003, 2005), White & Dahl (2007), Berger & Heath (2007), Miller, D. (2005)

**Mechanisms supported:**
- MM-10 (Material Culture Density): Direct — Muniz & O'Guinn (2001), Schouten & McAlexander (1995)
- MM-18 (Participatory Culture): Schau et al. (2009) 12 distinct tribal value creation practices
- BAD rubric: Five-tier Brand Anchor Density scoring
- Wrong Brand Penalty system: White & Dahl (2007) d=0.27 peripheral, d=0.55–0.70 core — grounds three-tier penalty structure

**Key published papers:**
- Muniz, A.M., & O'Guinn, T.C. (2001). Brand community. *Journal of Consumer Research*, 27, 412–432.
- Schouten, J.W., & McAlexander, J.H. (1995). Subcultures of consumption. *Journal of Consumer Research*, 22, 43–61.
- Schau, H.J., et al. (2009). How brand community practices create value. *Journal of Marketing*, 73, 30–51.
- White, K., & Dahl, D.W. (2007). Are all out-groups created equal? *Journal of Consumer Research*, 34, 525–536.
- Berger, J., & Heath, C. (2007). Where consumers diverge from others. *Journal of Consumer Research*, 34, 121–134. (3:1 behavioral avoidance ratio)

---

### Discipline 9: Behavioral Economics and Terror Management Theory (TMT)

**Core insight:** Decisions are not made by rational calculation. Loss aversion, mortality salience, deadline effects, and negativity dominance systematically bend choice in ways that the TSE must account for. The most important finding for scoring: Kahneman and Tversky's λ≈2.0 (loss aversion coefficient, replicated across approximately 100 studies) means contamination signals suppress conversion approximately twice as powerfully as equivalent positive signals amplify it.

This asymmetry is not a psychological curiosity — it is the architectural reason why the contamination system (CSS) exists as a separate module rather than as a factor inside the main score. Contamination is a different type of signal than identity alignment; it cannot be offset by adding more positive signals.

**Key researchers:** Kahneman & Tversky (1979), Burke et al. (2010), Ariely & Wertenbroch (2002), Slovic (1993), Baumeister et al. (2001), Fehr & Gächter (2000)

**Mechanisms supported:**
- CSS (Contamination Severity Scale): CSS-2 directly from Kahneman/Tversky λ≈2.0
- TSSM: Burke et al. (2010) TMT meta-analysis grounds the +1.0 acute ceiling
- BAC: Slovic (1993) and Kim et al. (2004) ground the 2–3 violation penalty at ×0.35
- Negativity dominance principle: Grounds all asymmetric penalty structures throughout the system

**Key published papers:**
- Kahneman, D., & Tversky, A. (1979). Prospect theory. *Econometrica*, 47, 263–291. (λ≈2.0, 95% CI [1.44–2.31], ~100 studies)
- Burke, B.L., et al. (2010). Two decades of Terror Management Theory. *Personality and Social Psychology Review*, 14, 155–195. (k=277, d=0.46)
- Baumeister, R.F., et al. (2001). Bad is stronger than good. *Review of General Psychology*, 5, 323–370.
- Slovic, P. (1993). Perceived risk, trust, and democracy. *Risk Analysis*, 13, 675–682.
- Kim, P.H., et al. (2004). Removing the shadow of suspicion. *Journal of Applied Psychology*, 89, 104–118.

---

### Discipline 10: Network Science

**Core insight:** Tribal information flows, reputation propagation, and cascade dynamics are network phenomena. Optimal Group Size (MM-16) is grounded in Dunbar's social brain hypothesis — 150 as the functional upper limit for groups maintaining mutual knowledge. The TSE V2 tribal viability window (5,000–500,000 active practitioners) sits above the Dunbar ceiling, which means viable consulting niches operate in the network layer above the direct-relationship layer: reputation propagates through chains of mutual recognition rather than direct familiarity.

**Key researchers:** Dunbar, R. (1992, 2012), Cialdini & Goldstein (2004), Watts & Strogatz (1998), Fagerstrøm (2020)

**Mechanisms supported:**
- MM-16 (Optimal Group Size): Dunbar number research establishing viability windows
- MM-19 (Social Proof Cascade): Network cascade dynamics — SP explains ~20–30% of incremental purchase intention variance
- SP component in TRS: Cialdini & Goldstein (2004) and Fagerstrøm (2020, d=0.43)

**Key published papers:**
- Dunbar, R.I.M. (1992). Neocortex size as a constraint on group size in primates. *Journal of Human Evolution*, 22, 469–493.
- Cialdini, R.B., & Goldstein, N.J. (2004). Social influence: Compliance and conformity. *Annual Review of Psychology*, 55, 591–621.

---

### Discipline 11: Military, Sports, and Religious Cohesion

**Core insight:** The highest-fidelity examples of tribal cohesion exist in military units, religious communities, and elite sports teams. These contexts provide calibration anchors for the upper end of every scoring dimension. CrossFit as a 1000/1000 reference benchmark incorporates elements from all three: military-grade physical discipline, religious community structure, and athletic competition hierarchy.

The Whitehouse et al. (2017) finding on extreme cooperation via shared dysphoric experiences is particularly relevant: units that have undergone extreme shared hardship show qualitatively different cooperation than units that have only undergone moderate challenge. This is the mechanism behind the TISF and MM-11 upper-score distinctions.

**Key researchers:** Whitehouse et al. (2014, 2017), Sosis & Alcorta (2003), Collins, R. (2004), Mullen et al. (1992)

**Mechanisms supported:**
- MM-04 (Initiation Structure): Military boot camp and religious rites as calibration anchors
- MM-08 (Synchronized Ritual): Military drill and religious liturgy as synchrony mechanisms
- MM-11 (Shared Suffering): Combat unit bonding as communitas example
- K-term boost: Mullen et al. (1992, k=137, d=0.38–0.68) grounds the ×1.25 territorial trust boost

**Key published papers:**
- Whitehouse, H., et al. (2017). The evolution of extreme cooperation via shared dysphoric experiences. *Scientific Reports*, 7, 44292.
- Collins, R. (2004). *Interaction Ritual Chains*. Princeton: Princeton University Press.
- Mullen, B., et al. (1992). Ingroup bias as a function of salience, relevance, and status. *European Journal of Social Psychology*, 22, 103–122. (k=137, d=0.38–0.68)

---

### Discipline 12: Honor Culture, Gift Economy, and Moral Psychology

**Core insight:** Different cultures and subcultures operate by different moral logic. Honor cultures (where reputation is actively defended and violations are met with confrontation) produce stronger implicit behavioral rules than dignity cultures. The finding with highest operational consequence for TSE V2: Nisbett & Cohen (1996) found implicit norm violations produce 4–5× stronger physiological response than explicit value challenges.

This is why the BAC modifier exists and why high-IBRD niches receive more severe violation penalties. In a culture-of-honor niche — trades, rural industries, certain professional services — the unwritten behavioral rules carry more behavioral weight than any stated creed. A consultant who violates an implicit rule in these contexts will not receive a warning; they will receive an immediate, permanent, and invisible rejection.

**Key researchers:** Nisbett & Cohen (1996), Leung & Cohen (2011), Graham et al. (2009), Douglas & Isherwood (1979), Haidt, J. (2001)

**Mechanisms supported:**
- MM-05 / MM-05B (Sacred Values + IBRD): Conditional 60/40 vs. 50/50 composite weighting
- MM-12 (Reputation Economy): Honor culture and gift economy dynamics ground this mechanism
- BAC modifier: Honor culture literature grounds the severity of violation penalties in IBRD ≥ 75 niches

**Key published papers:**
- Nisbett, R.E., & Cohen, D. (1996). *Culture of Honor*. Boulder: Westview Press.
- Graham, J., et al. (2009). Liberals and conservatives rely on different sets of moral foundations. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*, 96, 1029–1046.
- Haidt, J. (2001). The emotional dog and its rational tail. *Psychological Review*, 108, 814–834.
- Leung, A.K., & Cohen, D. (2011). Within- and between-culture variation. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*, 100, 507–526. (r=0.41)

## Part 4: Key Scientific Findings — The Most Important Mechanisms in Detail

### 4.1 Why CSS-2 = 0.75 (The Kahneman/Tversky Grounding)

The CSS-2 multiplier (×0.75 for "moderate contamination — fear and recognition roughly equal") is the most strongly grounded single value in the entire CSS system. Its derivation is unusually direct.

Kahneman and Tversky's loss aversion coefficient (λ≈2.0), replicated across approximately 100 studies with 95% CI [1.44–2.31], establishes that losses feel roughly twice as powerful as equivalent gains. This is one of the most replicated findings in behavioral economics and is domain-general — it operates in B2B creator decisions as robustly as in any other domain.

For a prospect encountering a CSS-2 contaminated niche, the psychological dynamics are:
- The recognition signal produces positive valence (approach motivation): "This consultant seems relevant"
- The fear/skepticism signal produces negative valence (avoidance motivation): "I've seen this type of thing before and it didn't work"
- When these are roughly equal in intensity (CSS-2 definition), the λ≈2.0 coefficient means the fear signal dominates

The result: 20–35% conversion suppression, which translates directly to a multiplier of 0.65–0.80. The 0.75 point estimate sits at the center of this empirically derived range. No assumption about B2B creators specifically is required; the conversion suppression flows directly from the Kahneman/Tversky coefficient.

This derivation also explains why CSS-2 is called the "navigable" contamination level. A 0.75 multiplier represents recoverable damage — a sub-category reframe (positioning within the niche to avoid the contamination source) can restore clean positioning. CSS-3 (×0.50, HYPOTHESIS) and CSS-4 (×0.00, terminal) are either difficult to navigate or structurally impossible to escape through repositioning alone.

**Practical consequence:** The CSS-2 boundary is the most operationally important decision point in the contamination system. Understanding whether a prospect perceives fear and recognition as roughly equal (CSS-2) vs. fear dominating (CSS-3) requires directly asking the tribe, not estimating from the outside. This is why the niche diagnostic process includes explicit contamination probing.

The research synthesis (raw/14_research_synthesis.md) provides supporting evidence from a different angle: the "too good to be true" phenomenon documented by Steinel, van Beest & van Dijk (2013) confirms that sufficiently extreme offers activate a fraud-detection heuristic that overrides desire for value. This is the same asymmetric loss-aversion mechanism in operation at the offer level. CSS measures the same phenomenon at the niche/category level.

---

### 4.2 Why MM-10B Is the Best-Grounded Mechanism in the System

MM-10B (Collective Experiential Timestamp Density) scores whether a tribe has shared temporal anchors — events that all members experienced simultaneously and that function as collective memory anchors. The scoring is categorical: 0 (no shared temporal events), 25 (rare sporadic events), 50 (regular events, distributed participation), 75 (strong seasonal calendar, majority participation), 100 (entire tribal calendar synchronized).

The 50-point score is designated a structural ANCHOR — one of only three ANchors in the entire system — because of an unusually direct derivation path that eliminates the main source of uncertainty in most TSE V2 values.

Most scoring mechanisms involve translation from academic constructs (social identity salience, loss aversion) into a B2B scoring framework. This translation step introduces uncertainty: the academic finding demonstrates that X is important, but by how much, in a scoring range of 0–100, applied to B2B creators? That question requires estimation.

MM-10B Score 50 does not require this translation. Two independent meta-analyses directly measure the prosocial effects of partial synchrony relative to full synchrony:
- Rennung & Goritz (2016): k=31 synchrony studies — partial synchrony produces 50–70% of full-synchrony prosocial effects
- Mogan et al. (2017): k=42 synchrony studies — confirms the 50–70% range independently

The TSE V2 rubric assigns Score 50 to "partial synchrony / distributed participation" and Score 100 to "full synchrony / entire calendar synchronized." The 50/100 ratio directly reads from the meta-analytic finding of 50–70% (conservatively modeled as 50%). The ratio is not an estimate. It is a direct reading from 73 synchrony studies.

This makes MM-10B Score 50 more reliably grounded than mechanisms requiring inferential translation from academic constructs. The confidence interval on this specific value is the tightest in the system: ±10 points at maximum, probably ±5 in practice.

The Score 100 case ("entire tribal calendar synchronized — every member does the same thing at the same time") is explicitly NOT YET REVIEWED. The CrossFit Open is the reference case: hundreds of thousands of people worldwide doing the same 5 workouts in the same 5-week window with publicly posted scores. Whether this produces effects genuinely double the partial-synchrony case or whether there are diminishing returns at very high synchrony has not been studied specifically. Score 100 is an architectural extrapolation pending direct empirical examination.

---

### 4.3 Why the Entitativity Cliff Exists (Campbell's Foundational Work)

The first S-curve cliff in the TSE conversion model — at TP 150–250 — exists because of a cognitive phenomenon documented by Campbell (1958) and subsequently validated by Lickel et al. (2000) across multiple study designs.

Campbell identified entitativity as a perception, not an objective property. Groups are not inherently "real" or "not real" — they are perceived as real or not-real based on several criteria evaluated together:

1. **Goal interdependence:** Do members share goals whose achievement depends on each other?
2. **Similarity:** Do members share observable characteristics that make the category coherent?
3. **Boundary clarity:** Is it clear who is in and who is out?
4. **Interaction history:** Have members actually engaged with each other over time?

When all four criteria are weak — as in "mindset coaches for entrepreneurs," where there are no shared goals, no shared occupational characteristics, no clear membership definition, and minimal shared interaction — the collection has near-zero entitativity. It is a demographic classification, not a tribe. Conversion behavior is indistinguishable from cold-prospect outreach, regardless of how compelling the content.

When all four criteria are strong — as in "CrossFit affiliate owners," who share the goal of sustaining methodology-driven gyms, practice the same physical method, have a very clear membership definition (you own an affiliate or you don't), and interact continuously through the affiliate network — entitativity is high. Members process each other automatically as tribal kin, extend trust pre-rationally, and respond to identity-congruent offers at dramatically higher rates.

**The cliff is a step-function, not a gradient.** This is what SCT predicts: there is a categorical shift between "processing this person as a unique individual" and "processing this person as a category prototype." Below the entitativity threshold, personal identity mode dominates — the prospect evaluates the consultant individually, requiring the full trust-building sequence. Above the threshold, social identity mode activates — the consultant is immediately categorized as "one of us" or "not one of us," and tribal recognition fires before any rational evaluation begins.

The practical consequence for scoring: a TP score of 180 and a TP score of 220 may look similar numerically, but they can sit on opposite sides of a cognitive cliff. The 180 niche requires full individual trust-building before conversion occurs. The 220 niche enables tribal recognition shortcuts. Content that would be ignored in the 180 niche produces the "holy shit that's me" recognition event in the 220 niche.

The exact TP range for the cliff (150–250) is a structural inference from the mechanism, not a direct empirical measurement. Real scoring data from 30+ niches is required to pin this range more precisely. The best current estimate is TP 200 ± 50. Any use of specific TP values near this range should carry this uncertainty explicitly.

---

### 4.4 Why Identity Fusion Creates Price Insensitivity (Swann et al.)

Price insensitivity in fused buyers is not irrationality. It is the logical consequence of identity economics.

Swann et al. (2009, 2012) established that identity fusion dissolves the self-group boundary. When personal identity and group identity fully merge, the fused individual's psychological welfare is coupled to the group's welfare. This is not a weak preference or strong identification — it is a structural change in how threats and opportunities are processed. Fused individuals experience the group's outcomes as their own.

Three downstream effects are documented across multiple independent study designs:

**Proxy agency (Swann & Buhrmester 2015):** Fused individuals feel personally accomplished by group members' achievements, even without direct participation. A CrossFitter feels pride when their box qualifies for Regionals even if they didn't compete. This makes the group's success a renewable personal resource — you can always access achievement through your tribe. This is the mechanism behind tribal evangelism: promoting the tribe's success feels like promoting your own success.

**Extreme pro-group behavior:** Fused members demonstrate behavioral patterns that social identity theory (mere identification) cannot explain — willingness to risk personal harm to protect the group, disproportionate resource sacrifice, and active recruitment of new members. Buhrmester et al. (2012) document that the transition from high identification to fusion predicts these extreme behaviors independently of identification level. Fusion is a categorical threshold, not just high identification.

**Price insensitivity as identity expression:** For a fused buyer considering a consultant from their tribe, the purchase is not a transaction. It is an act of identity affirmation, group maintenance, and proxy agency. Refusing to pay the tribal rate would feel like undermining something they are. This is why fused buyers do not comparison-shop: comparison shopping implies the purchase could be made from outside the tribe, which is cognitively incompatible with fusion.

The second S-curve cliff in the TSE conversion model (TP 700–800) corresponds to the onset of identity fusion at scale. Below TP 700, members may have high identification but not fusion — they see themselves as belonging to the tribe. Above TP 800, meaningful proportions of the buyer pool are fused — they are the tribe. The difference in conversion behavior is categorical. It is the same kind of categorical shift as the entitativity cliff — not a quantitative increase but a qualitative change in how the purchasing decision is processed.

**The TPC connection:** The Tribal Payment Context Score (TPC) mechanism captures the fusion dynamic at the offer level. Nelissen & Meijers (2011, d=0.68) and Sundie et al. (2011, d=0.45–0.65 across 9 studies) confirm that paying the tribal price functions as a costly signal of group commitment. The Q3 question (+0.50: "paying the tribal price signals commitment to the tribe") was upgraded in the Phase 5 rebuild specifically because this finding from the costly signaling / status-signaling literature was clearer and larger than originally acknowledged.

---

### 4.5 Why Costly Signals Work (Zahavi's Principle Applied to B2B)

Zahavi's 1975 Handicap Principle is the theoretical foundation for four distinct TSE V2 mechanisms: MM-04 (Initiation Structure), MM-21 (Linguistic Register Depth), the IFS rubric's brand anchor scoring, and the TISF (Tribal Identity Signal Failure) penalty system.

The core insight: in any signaling system where cheating is possible and costly to detect, the only credible signals are those whose production requires the very qualities they claim to demonstrate. A signal that costs nothing to fake communicates nothing, because anyone with an incentive to deceive will fake it.

Applied to group membership: the vocabulary a consultant uses in cold outreach is a costly signal only if incorrect vocabulary use is immediately detectable by the target. CrossFit affiliate owners will notice within the first line of an email whether the sender uses "WOD" correctly, knows the benchmark girl workouts, and uses "box" rather than "gym." The detection is not deliberate scrutiny — it is automatic pattern matching that fires before conscious evaluation begins. This is why MM-01 scoring begins with vocabulary authenticity: not because vocabulary is the most important tribal dimension, but because it is the first gating mechanism the prospect's neural pattern-matching applies.

**Why humor is the strongest costly signal in content:** Oring's (1992) schema-gated humor theory explains why insider humor is structurally immune to imitation. The joke requires the same tacit knowledge to find funny as it requires to produce. You cannot fake understanding an insider joke — either you get it or you don't, and your response to it is an involuntary signal of your status. Producing a joke that lands requires knowing the hierarchy, the shared grievances, the language patterns, and the timing norms of the community at a level that cannot be acquired from external research. This is why the IFS rubric assigns the highest point value (+15 points, ANCHOR) to "insider humor deployed correctly without explanation" — it is the most credible tribal credential precisely because it is the hardest to fake.

The hierarchy of imitation resistance, from easiest to hardest to fake:
1. Vocabulary (lexical register): Acquirable from a glossary in hours
2. Technical references: Acquirable from Wikipedia and documentation
3. Explicit values and stated beliefs: Acquirable by listening to what the tribe says publicly
4. Pragmatic complaint patterns: Requires genuine community immersion to deploy correctly
5. Implicit behavioral rule compliance: Requires lived experience to internalize
6. Insider humor: Requires tacit knowledge that cannot be extracted without being there

TSE V2 weights these inversely — the hardest to fake receives the highest weight. This was the primary motivation for the MM-21 weight rebuild (Lexical 40% → 30%; Pragmatic 30% → 35%; Humor 30% → 35%).

**The irreversibility finding (Sosis & Bressler 2003):** Analysis of 200 years of US commune data confirmed that what drives cohesion is not the cost per se, but the irreversibility of the commitment. Communes requiring reversible costs (dues, optional dietary restrictions) dissolved faster than communes requiring irreversible commitments (property donation, permanent relocation). Applied to MM-04 scoring: a certification that can be abandoned with no economic consequence scores lower than a business investment (like gym ownership) that cannot be unwound without significant disruption. The costly signal becomes maximally credible when exit requires sacrificing the investment made to enter.

---

## Part 5: What's Validated, What's an Estimate, and What's a Hypothesis

This section provides explicit epistemic transparency for practitioners using TSE V2 scores in real decisions.

### Empirically Validated (Use with High Confidence)

**The Identity, Cultural, Linguistic, and Recognition & Trust frameworks:** These four engines have been applied in client work over years of consulting practice. The frameworks predate TSE V2 and have accumulated pragmatic validation through use. They are not validated in the academic sense — there are no controlled trials — but they have been stress-tested against real-world tribal assessment across dozens of niches. Patterns that fail empirically have been removed; patterns that reliably predict tribal recognition have been retained.

**The TSE V1 conversion gap (13.6×):** Six scored niches showing a 13.6× gap between low-scoring and high-scoring niches on conversion metrics. This is the strongest empirical anchor in the entire system. It does not validate the specific mechanism weights — it validates the overall construct: that whatever TSE V1 is measuring predicts conversion at the level this gap implies. The T1 mechanism gap (15–18×) exceeds the overall gap, confirming that Tier 1 gateway mechanisms are the correct primary lever.

**The 60/30/10 tier weighting structure:** Stryker (1980) identity salience hierarchy establishes that gateway mechanisms are primary activation thresholds. The empirical validation data from six scored niches confirms T1 primacy: the T1 gap (15–18×) exceeds the overall TP gap (13.6×). The weighting is validated directionally; the specific percentages (60% vs. 55% vs. 65%) are design choices within a validated structure.

**CSS-2 multiplier (×0.75):** Directly derivable from Kahneman/Tversky λ≈2.0. The domain generality of this coefficient (replicated in hundreds of studies across dozens of domains) means no B2B-specific validation is needed. This is the TSE's most strongly grounded quantitative value.

**MM-10B Score 50 (ANCHOR):** Direct reading from 73 synchrony studies (Rennung & Goritz 2016 k=31, Mogan et al. 2017 k=42). No inference required.

**TSSM acute ceiling (+1.0):** Burke et al. (2010) k=277 TMT meta-analysis provides anchor-level support. The acute window (<6 months) ceiling is structurally derived; the +1.0 ceiling value is an architectural anchor explicitly not representing the best empirical estimate (+0.70 is more conservative).

---

### Well-Reasoned Estimates (Use with Explicit Uncertainty Labels)

**TTSM multipliers (1.18, 1.30, 1.35 for 2, 3, 4 identity layers):** The concave shape is validated — more identity layers produce diminishing additional returns. The specific values are design choices within a validated directional structure. Forehand et al. (2002) and Gaither & Sommers (2013) support the direction; the specific increments require empirical calibration against multi-layer niche scoring data.

**Entitativity cliff location (TP 150–250):** The existence of a cliff is validated by Campbell (1958) and Lickel et al. (2000). The TP mapping (150–250) is an architectural inference. The cliff exists; its exact TP position requires 30+ scored niches to pin.

**Identity fusion threshold (TP 700–800):** Swann et al. establish the VFS threshold (18–25/35) for fusion. The TP 700–800 mapping is an architectural inference. Best estimate TP 725, range [600–850].

**Score-to-outcome conversion multiples:** The 13.6× V1 gap is empirical. The 15–22× range at TP 850+ is an upper-bound architectural estimate derived by compounding five independent mechanisms (DMA, TTSM, identity fusion, price insensitivity, referral cascade). Each mechanism is individually grounded; their joint product has not been directly measured. Treat as directionally correct and practically conservative.

**BAC multiplier tiers (0.60/0.75/0.88/1.00/1.10):** The categorical difference between surface and tribal brand connection is validated by Escalas & Bettman (2003, r=0.31 vs. r=0.62). The specific tier values within this range are design choices requiring calibration.

---

### Explicit Hypotheses (Do Not Present as Established)

**CSS-3 (×0.50):** FLAG_AS_HYPOTHESIS. Grounded directionally by Baumeister (2001) negativity dominance (negative signals 2–8× more powerful than positive). The specific 0.50 value has no direct B2B analog and the mechanism has not been tested in B2B creator contexts. Confidence: LOW-MEDIUM. Do not present as established — this value requires empirical calibration before production use. (Note: previously misplaced under "Well-Reasoned Estimates" — corrected in QA pass T34.)

**MM-05_composite weighting (60%/40% explicit/implicit):** FLAG_AS_HYPOTHESIS. Graham et al. (2009) motivates the direction; no B2B niche study tests the split. In high-IBRD niches the split may be closer to 50/50 per Nisbett & Cohen (1996), but this is a recommendation, not a finding.

**Super-band score boundaries:** The TP ranges defining Non-tribal, Pre-tribal, Weak, Mid, Strong, Fusion, and Elite bands are architecturally derived. They have coherent internal logic but have not been validated against tracked conversion outcomes by band. Phase 6 empirical calibration should establish whether the boundaries hold in practice.

**Digital TP ceiling (650–750):** Architecturally derived from MM-14A=0 (no physical territory) and MM-14B cap of 75 (digital ceiling). Supported directionally by Media Richness Theory and Ellison et al. (2007). The specific 650–750 range is a hypothesis until direct comparison of digital-only vs. physical-plus-digital tribes is completed.

**ORTD weights (0.15 in modified IRS) and BAD weights (0.20 in modified IRS):** NO_SCIENCE_EXISTS. These values must not appear in production scoring. They are explicitly EXPLORATORY flags requiring 50+ niche calibration studies before any confidence can be assigned.

---

## Part 6: The Research Synthesis Layer — Buyer Psychology Grounding

The 12 academic disciplines above ground the tribal scoring mechanisms directly. A parallel body of evidence from market research and buyer psychology grounds the offer viability layer that interacts with tribal scoring. The key findings from raw/14_research_synthesis.md are summarized here.

### The Asymmetric Offer Attractiveness Effect

Steinel, van Beest & van Dijk (2013, *Group Processes & Intergroup Relations*, Vol. 17(2)) demonstrated in three controlled studies that participants *rejected* high offers more often than equal offers when information was asymmetric. Being "too generous" triggered the inference that the other party knew something the prospect didn't — a protective cognitive response manifesting as rejection.

This is the direct experimental demonstration of the "too good to be true" effect. A sufficiently extreme offer activates a fraud-detection heuristic that overrides the desire for value. Specifically, extreme claims are effective *only* when combined with high advertiser credibility (Goldberg & Hartwick 1990). For unknown or new brands, extreme claims actively harm credibility versus moderate claims.

**Kahneman dual-process connection:** System 1 (fast, automatic, pattern-matching) scans offers against known patterns. It compares to category norms. It triggers a "scam alert" heuristic if anomaly is detected. This results in dismissal without detailed evaluation. System 2 (slow, deliberate, analytical) is only engaged if System 1 doesn't flag an anomaly. Unbelievable offers trigger System 1 before System 2 ever engages. No amount of testimonials, evidence, or guarantees in the body copy rescues a claim that failed System 1 pattern-matching in the headline.

**TSE V2 connection:** A tribe with high CSS contamination is pre-primed to trigger System 1 rejection regardless of offer quality. This is why CSS is multiplicative, not additive: contamination at the category level means the fraud-detection heuristic fires before any individual offer evaluation begins.

### The Trust Utility Formula

Expected utility theory formalizes why unbelievable offers fail:

```
Trust Utility = P(the promised outcome is deliverable) × Value(promised outcome)
```

These are multiplicative. When a marketer increases Value dramatically (by adding guarantees, bonuses, and extreme promises), they may simultaneously decrease P(outcome) — because the extreme value claim triggers skepticism. The net Trust Utility can fall even as stated value rises.

Research example:
- Agency offering "3 qualified appointments/week" for $1,500/month
  - P(deliverable) = 0.6, Value = 8 → Trust Utility = **4.8**
- Same agency offering "30 guaranteed appointments or we pay $5,000" for $1,500/month
  - Value increases to 15
  - P drops to 0.15 (seems impossible/suspicious)
  - Trust Utility = **2.25** — *lower despite higher stated value*

**Implication for tribal scoring:** High-skepticism tribes (sophisticated B2B buyers, experienced entrepreneurs, tech workers who have been sold bad coaching) have lower P thresholds. For these tribes, the Trust Utility formula is operating at a lower P baseline — meaning the same offer claims produce lower Trust Utility than for less-skeptical audiences. TSE V2's tribal skepticism layer (which is embedded in the CSS and IBRD mechanisms) reflects this: tribes that have been burned by repeated failed promises require credibility-first positioning, not value-first positioning.

### Business Failure Data Grounding

The CB Insights post-mortem analysis of 101 startup failures (2021) found that 42% of failed startups cited "no market need" as the primary cause of failure — the single largest failure reason, above running out of cash (29%), wrong team (23%), or competition (19%).

This data grounds a critical claim in TSE V2: **problem existence ≠ active shopping.** Niche scoring must distinguish between "this tribe has the pain" and "this tribe is actively seeking relief." A tribe where the D variable (Latent Demand) is low is a structurally unviable niche regardless of tribal density. A high TP score in a D=0 niche produces enthusiastic engagement with no conversion — the tribe responds to the content but has no behavior pointing toward purchase.

BLS Business Employment Dynamics survival data further contextualizes the stakes: approximately 50% of new businesses fail by Year 5, with 82% of failures attributable to cash flow problems (U.S. Bank study, Jessie Hagen). Cash flow problems are often downstream of niche selection problems — a niche where payment normalisation (P) is low produces slow, difficult sales cycles and inconsistent revenue before being identified as a structural niche issue.

---

- Leung, A.K., & Cohen, D. (2011). Within- and between-culture variation. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*, 100, 507–526.
- Haidt, J. (2001). The emotional dog and its rational tail. *Psychological Review*, 108, 814–834.

### Environmental Psychology and Place Identity

- Scannell, L., & Gifford, R. (2010). Defining place attachment. *Journal of Environmental Psychology*, 30, 1–10.
- Tonge, J., et al. (2022). Place identity meta-analysis. (k=80, N>20,000, r=0.28, CI [.24,.32])
- Mullen, B., et al. (1992). Ingroup bias as a function of salience. *European Journal of Social Psychology*, 22, 103–122.

### Pricing, Status, and Social Proof

- Mazumdar, T., et al. (2005). Reference price research. *Journal of Marketing*, 69, 84–102.
- Völckner, F., & Hofmann, J. (2007). The price-quality relationship. *Marketing Letters*, 18, 181–196.
- Nelissen, R.M.A., & Meijers, M.H.C. (2011). Social benefits of luxury brands. *Evolution and Human Behavior*, 32, 343–355. (d=0.68)
- Sundie, J.M., et al. (2011). Peacocks, Porsches, and Thorsten Veblen. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*, 100, 664–680.
- Cialdini, R.B., & Goldstein, N.J. (2004). Social influence: Compliance and conformity. *Annual Review of Psychology*, 55, 591–621.
- Kim, Y.K., & Lee, K.H. (2019). Authenticity in luxury brand consumption. *Journal of Business Research*, 99, 134–145.
- Reinikainen, H., et al. (2020). Parasocial relationships and credibility. *Journal of Marketing*, 84, 73–88. (r=0.42)

### Buyer Psychology Research (from Research Synthesis)

- Steinel, W., van Beest, I., & van Dijk, E. (2013). Being too generous to look honest. *Group Processes & Intergroup Relations*, 17(2).
- Goldberg, M.E., & Hartwick, J. (1990). The effects of advertiser reputation and extremity of advertising claim on advertising effectiveness. *Journal of Consumer Research*, 17, 172–179.

### Epistemological Foundation

- Dawes, R.M. (1979). The robust beauty of improper linear models. *American Psychologist*, 34, 571–582. — Grounds the decision to replace continuous RCD formula with 3-tier gate.
